Figure 1. Blind perspective-n-point with MomentPnP. The inputs are (a) an unordered set of 2D SIFT points [49] in a Sacr´ e-Cœur image from the Phototourism dataset [37, 69], tracked by its structure-from-motion reconstruction [64], (b) the unordered 3D point set of those tracks, and known camera intrinsics K. ## Abstract 

The blind perspective-n-point (PnP) problem is to estimate the pose of a calibrated camera from 2D and 3D point sets with unknown correspondences. We show that, for complete, noiseless observations, fourteen permutation-invariant im-age moments up to order four identify a fixed camera pose for almost every 3D point set containing at least twenty points. Our learning-free method, MomentPnP, matches these mo-ments between the observed 2D points and the projected 3D points to initialize a camera pose without correspon-dences and refines the pose and correspondence weights using expectation–maximization. Across the test sets of three benchmarks, our method reduces median rotation error by 

5–170 × relative to the best learned baseline, both with and without outliers. We recover accurate poses even with as many outliers as inliers. On dense ETH3D scans, pose ac-curacy improves as the point sets grow, even as individual correspondences become harder to resolve. 

## 1. Introduction 

The blind PnP problem is to estimate a calibrated camera’s pose from unordered 2D points and 3D model points, without given correspondences [54] (Fig. 1). It enables localization from geometry alone when compatible visual descriptors are deliberately omitted [82] or unavailable, as for laser scans [7]. The sets must still share scene structure: inlier 2D points are projections of model points under a common camera pose, while either set may contain unmatched points. Known pairs would give the pose by PnP [44], but the num-ber of possible pairings grows superpolynomially with the set sizes [24]. Local methods alternate pose estimation and correspon-dence updates, making their success sensitive to initializa-tion [11, 54]. Global methods search pose space by branch and bound [4, 5, 7], with computational cost driven by the re-gions that must be examined and excluded. Learned methods predict correspondences from coordinates, using information acquired from training data [6, 48, 82]. These limitations motivate estimating the pose from the whole point sets before resolving matches. Because a known 3D model limits how its points can appear in an image, feature averages of the projected model constrain the pose without any pairing of points [60, 72]. Mean and covariance describe position and spread, but dis-tinct projections can share them (Fig. 2). Higher-order mo-ments, averages of products of point coordinates [19, 34], capture shape beyond the mean and covariance [31] and can give the pose of planar point sets. Whether a fixed number of moments determines a perspective camera pose without pairing points was, to our knowledge, open. 

Contributions. We introduce MomentPnP (Sec. 4), alearning-free blind PnP solver that estimates the camera pose before the correspondences. It adjusts the six pose parameters until averages of bounded Fourier features over the projected model match those over the observations, after subtracting the expected effect of outliers in both sets [23]. 1arXiv:2611.20797v1 [cs.CV] 16 Nov 2026 This comparison pairs no points. On complete inputs, it matches fourteen polynomial moments, and we prove that noiseless moments determine the pose under perspective for almost every model of at least twenty points (Theorem 1). With outliers and noise, finitely many Fourier frequencies identify the pose and bound its error by the residual plus sampling and noise terms (Theorem 2). 

Empirical results. On released blind PnP benchmarks, Mo-mentPnP has a 5 to 170 times smaller median rotation error than the best learned baseline, and 82 to 97% of frames are within 1◦ with as many outliers as inliers (Tab. 1). On rendered ETH3D laser scans [65], its median rotation error falls to 0.00007 ◦ at one million points (Fig. 8). In full scene reconstructions, it localizes 35 of 39 ETH3D images within 

1◦ (Tab. A18). 

## 2. Related work 

Blind PnP. Methods differ in how they couple pose and cor-respondences. Hypothesize-and-test methods such as P3P-RANSAC [18] sample triples of candidate pairs, but correct triples become unlikely as the sets grow. Local methods such as SoftPOSIT [11] and BlindPnP [54] jointly estimate pose and soft correspondences from an initial pose or pose prior, as do recent refinements [3, 70]. Global methods such as GOPAC [7] and GOSMA [5] search pose space by branch and bound [4, 29, 77], at a cost growing with the search domain. Learned methods such as Deep Blind PnP [48] and BPnPNet [6] predict correspondences from coordinates and then solve PnP. GoMatch [82] does so for localization against retrieved views, and MinCD-PnP [1] adds color im-ages and surface normals. All rely on correspondences, a pose prior or pose search. 

Point-set registration. Registering point sets of equal di-mension also couples transformations and correspondences. Softassign [20], EM-ICP [21] and coherent point drift [55] alternate between soft correspondences and a transforma-tion, which we adopt for refinement under perspective pro-jection. Moment-based methods avoid correspondences by comparing moments. They have been used for planar ob-ject pose [72], affine registration [31] and planar shapes under homographies [16]. Sketching methods also average sines and cosines over whole datasets [23]. Because these features are bounded, estimators that match their averages remain accurate when a fraction of the data are outliers [8]. Correspondence-free pose estimation (CFPE) [60] comes closest to our setting by matching averages of nonlinear fea-tures under perspective projection, but its planar experiments start near the true pose. We estimate a perspective camera pose from feature averages of whole point sets, without a pose prior, with outliers in both sets, before matching points. 

Kernel mean embeddings. Averages of feature maps rep-resent a distribution as a kernel mean embedding, and maxi-mum mean discrepancy measures the distance between two embeddings [22]. For characteristic kernels, equal embed-dings imply equal distributions [71]. Our feature averages are embeddings of this kind, but we need identification only among the projections of one known model. Sec. 4.3 proves it from fourteen moments on complete inputs and from finitely many Fourier features with outliers. 

Pose verification. A fitted pose can be wrong in two ways. For planar targets, two poses can give nearly the same image even with known correspondences [58, 66]. Without corre-spondences, a wrong pose can also place projections near many observations by chance. Desolneux et al. [14] judge a structure by how often it would arise at random, and Moisan and Stival [53] apply this test to two-view matching. We check alignment against randomly placed projections and compare each accepted pose with its half-turn alternatives (Sec. 4.1). 

## 3. Problem statement 

Input & camera. We are given n 2D points (observations) 

U = {ui}ni=1 ⊂ R2, N model points Y = {yj }Nj=1 ⊂ R3

and the intrinsic matrix K of the camera, with zero skew and positive focal lengths. No correspondences between the two point sets are given [6, 54]. A pose ξ = ( R, t) ∈ SO(3) ×R3

maps a model point y to camera coordinates x = Ry + t.If its depth x3 is positive, its image proj( x) is the first two entries of Kx/x 3 [27]. We write pj (ξ) = proj( Ryj + t).

Correspondences, outliers & noise. The correspondences form an unknown map σ : {1, . . . , n } → { 1, . . . , N } ∪ {∅}. We set σ(i) = j when observation i is the image of model point j, and σ(i) = ∅ when it is the image of no model point. Distinct observations correspond to distinct model points. The m corresponding pairs are inliers, and the remaining n − m observations and N − m model points are outliers [6, 82]. The number m is not known. Complete inputs have n = N = m. At the true pose ξ⋆, every inlier model point has positive depth, and we model each inlier observation as 

ui = pσ(i)(ξ⋆) + wi, wi ∼ N (0 , s 2I2), (1) with i.i.d. noise wi of unknown standard deviation s pixels per coordinate. This noise also approximately absorbs model errors [27]. 

Task. Blind PnP is the task of estimating, from U, Y and 

K, the true pose ξ⋆ and the correspondences σ of Eq. (1). 

## 4. MomentPnP 

In blind PnP (Sec. 3), MomentPnP estimates the camera pose from observations, a 3D model and the intrinsics, without correspondences. In general, both point sets contain outliers. Complete inputs, without outliers, are an exception that MomentPnP treats separately when this is known. 24.1. Preliminaries 

Polynomial Moments. Moments are statistical averages of products of point coordinates, and their order g is the number of factors [19, 34]. Averages need no pairing of points, so earlier correspondence-free methods use moments [16, 31, 72], and we use orders one to four, Mg . Orders three and four capture asymmetry and concentration, which separate shapes with equal centroid and covariance (Fig. 2a–d). For an observation u in pixels, the calibrated point ucal 

consists of the first two coordinates of K−1(u⊤, 1) ⊤, where 

K is the intrinsic matrix [27]. The affine map h(u) = (ucal − ¯ucal )/r cal centers calibrated points at the mean ¯ucal 

of the calibrated observations and divides by their root-mean-square radius rcal , which keeps the coordinates well conditioned [28]. We write z = h(u) for the normalized point, and the same map normalizes the projection pj (ξ) of each model point at a pose ξ (Sec. 3). Stacking the fourteen monomials of orders one to four of a normalized point z gives the feature vector 

ϕ4(z) = ( z1, z 2, z 21 , z 1z2, z 22 , . . . , z 42 ) ∈ R14 . Its aver-ages μU = 1

ϕ4(h(pj (ξ))) over the projections are the moments of both point sets, and on complete inputs they determine the pose (Theorem 1). 

Trigonometric moments. Monomial features fail with out-liers, because one distant outlier can dominate the mo-ments (Fig. 2e). Like sketching methods [23], MomentPnP therefore uses weighted cosines and sines ϕF (z) =(cos k⊤z, sin k⊤z)k∈F over a finite set F of frequencies. 1

The Fourier feature averages μU,F and μY,F (ξ) are defined like μU and μY (ξ), with ϕF and hF in place of ϕ4 and h.They are the trigonometric moments [51] of the point dis-tribution. Because the features are bounded, one outlier has a bounded effect, and low frequencies describe the coarse layout as low orders do [35, 38]. As in the benchmark protocol [6], we consider image outliers are spread uniformly over the rectangle that bounds the observations, and model outliers through the box that bounds the 3D model. On average, an image outlier adds 

μU,0, the average of ϕF over the rectangle, and a model outlier adds μY,0(ξ), the average of ϕF over the projections of points spread through the box. With a fraction η of outliers in each point set, μU,F and μY,F (ξ) mix the inlier averages with μU,0 and μY,0(ξ). The inlier averages agree at the true pose, so the expected difference there is η(μU,0 − μY,0(ξ)) .

over the pose ξ and η. The objective is quadratic in the unknown η, so the best η at each pose has a closed form. On complete inputs, η = 0 and no outlier can dominate the moments, so they replace the Fourier feature averages, and we minimize L4(ξ) = ∥μU − μY (ξ)∥2 (Theorem 1). 

Expectation–maximization (EM). EM [13, 21, 55] then refines the pose with every observation, each explained as a uniform outlier over the rectangle or a noisy projection of a model point in front of the camera (Fig. 3c). A refined pose is correct only if the observations lie much closer to their nearest projections than to the next, which we test against randomly placed projections [14, 53]. 

Definition 2 (Association level) . For observation i, let 

di, 1 ≤ di, 2 ≤ · · · be the distances to the projections in increasing order. For projections placed as a homogeneous Poisson process of any density, each Ei = −ℓ log(1 −

d2

> i, 1

/d 2

> i,ℓ +1

), which compares the nearest distance with the local spacing over ℓ ≤ 8 neighbors, would be unit exponen-tial [9]. Observation i is associated when Ei ≤ π/ 16 , that is, when its nearest projection lies within about a quarter of the average spacing. The association level is the probability under random placement, with independent observations, of at least the observed number of associated observations.

(b) Moment matching refines the best cameras, raising the cutoff from 4 to 20. (c) EM refines fits with soft correspondences. the expected effect of outliers in both sets, which pairs no points. Because the objective has many local minima [57], Step 1 supplies starting poses. Because averages ignore the individual observations, Step 3 refines the matched pose until it explains each observation on its own. Because a wrong pose can still align with the observations by chance, or through an ambiguity that averages cannot resolve, Step 4 checks the refined pose before it is returned. 

Step 1: Camera initialization. Without a pose prior, the image may show any part of the model, so MomentPnP first places candidate cameras throughout it. The candidates combine camera centers on a grid over an enlarged bounding box of the model with viewing directions over the sphere and roll angles about each. We score each candidate, as in GOSMA [5], by how much more densely its projections cluster at the observations than around them, measured by the ratio of a narrow to a broad smoothing of the projections, and refine the best candidates by this score. 

Step 2: Moment matching. Step 2 fits the pose by mini-mizing the moment-matching objective of Definition 1. For a candidate camera ξ, a taper fades out projections outside the rectangle of the observations, so only the model points that project near the observations contribute to μY,F (ξ), and the average changes smoothly as points enter or leave the view [26]. Starting from the candidates with distinct orien-tations and the lowest LF , Levenberg–Marquardt [45, 52] minimizes LF over the six pose parameters under perspec-tive projection. The fit proceeds from coarse to fine. The cutoff KF rises from 4 to 20 in five stages, and after each stage only the fits of lowest LF with distinct orientations continue. The best fits go to EM (Fig. 3b). 

Step 3: Expectation–maximization refinement. For re-finement we adapt EM-ICP and coherent point drift [21, 55] to perspective projection. EM refines each fit with the mix-ture of uniform outliers and noisy projections of Sec. 4.1, and estimates the noise standard deviation and the outlier probability together with the pose (Fig. 3c). The refined poses are then ranked by the likelihood of the observations as a Poisson process of uniform outliers and Gaussian peaks at the projections [9]. A refined pose can still be wrong in two ways. It can place projections near many observations by chance, or it can be one of the poses that give nearly the same image as the truth. Step 4 tests for both. 

Step 4: Alignment check and pose ambiguity resolution. 

An observation is matched when its nearest projection ex-plains it better than the outlier term does. The alignment check accepts a refined pose when enough observations are matched, their median ratio di, 1/d i, 2 is small, and the as-sociation level of Definition 2 is very small. Acceptance does not settle the pose of a nearly symmetric or shallow 4model, because other poses give nearly the same image and the averages cannot separate them (Sec. 4.3). For a dis-tant camera [12], the poses that give the image the same mean and covariance as an accepted pose with rotation R

are its seven alternatives RH k and DRH k in Fig. 3d, where 

H1, H 2, H 3 turn the model by 180 ◦ about the principal axes of its matched points, H0 is the identity, and D turns the camera by 180 ◦ about the optical axis. That these seven are all the poses the mean and covariance cannot separate is proved in App. A.7. 2 Which of them EM then prefers is not proved, but on the benchmarks 63 of the 64 poses that an alternative replaced end within 1◦ (App. C.7). MomentPnP therefore refines the most likely accepted pose and its seven alternatives by EM under perspective, checks each, and re-turns the most likely of them. Without an accepted pose, the search widens and then returns the most likely refined pose. At the returned pose, each matched observation corresponds to its nearest projection, and every other observation is an outlier, ˆσ(i) = ∅. When several observations share a nearest projection, only the closest keeps it, so distinct observations correspond to distinct model points. The refit of an accepted pose to its correspondences is given in App. B. 

4.3. Uniqueness and accuracy of the pose 

Moment matching recovers the pose only if two properties hold. On ideal complete inputs, its objective vanishes at the true pose alone (uniqueness). With noise and outliers, its minimizer stays within a bounded distance of the true pose (accuracy). Theorem 1 proves uniqueness for complete inputs. Theorem 2 builds on it to prove both for LF .

Uniqueness for complete inputs. On complete noiseless inputs, L4 vanishes at the true pose and, for a symmetric model such as a cube, at a second pose. Such models are exceptional. Equal moments at a second pose impose poly-nomial equations on the model points, and a dimension count shows that almost no model of N ≥ 20 points satisfies them. 

Theorem 1 (Pose uniqueness for complete inputs) . Fix the true pose ξ⋆ of a calibrated camera. For almost every model of N ≥ 20 points in front of the camera, every pose that keeps the points in front of the camera and gives their pro-jections the same fourteen moments as ξ⋆ equals ξ⋆.

Only the true pose therefore has L4 = 0 , which justifies minimizing L4.3

Accuracy of the Fourier fit. On ideal inputs, zero LF over all frequencies determines the inlier projections and the out-lier fraction, since the cosine and sine averages uniquely determine a distribution [38]. The pose is then determined   

> 2When the principal variances of the model differ, these alternatives include every pose that a symmetry of the model produces, and for a planar model they include the opposite tilt [58, 66].
> 3The twenty-point bound serves the proof and is not an operational limit. MomentPnP recovers poses within 1◦from 8 and 16 noiseless points.

by Theorem 1. Observation noise and finite sampling of the outliers generally make the loss nonzero even at the true pose. Theorem 2 bounds the error at a finite cutoff: for every cutoff KF above a threshold, the loss and the pertur-bations bound the distance dist between any candidate (ξ, η )

and the truth (ξ⋆, η ⋆). This distance combines the rotation, translation and image-outlier fraction errors. 

Theorem 2 (Accuracy of the Fourier fit) . Let the outliers be drawn independently and uniformly from the rectangle of the observations and the bounding box of the model, and let a compact set Θ of pairs (ξ, η ) contain (ξ⋆, η ⋆), with every pose keeping the box in front of the camera and η ∈ [0 , 1) .For almost every model of m ≥ 20 inlier points and every cutoff KF above a threshold K0, there is a constant CF such that, for each δ ∈ (0 , 1) , with probability at least 1 − δ over the outliers, every (ξ, η ) ∈ Θ satisfies 

dist ≤ CF

 p LF (ξ, η ) + ∆ δ

.

The error term ∆δ adds the sampling and grid errors of the outlier averages, the noise error and twice the difference of the image and model outlier fractions. 

At the true pair, √LF is at most ∆δ , so the minimizing pair lies within 2CF ∆δ of the truth, and LF can select fits for EM. The constant CF is large when LF grows slowly away from the truth on ideal inputs. The error term measures the departure from this ideal, and only its sampling part, which shrinks as the point sets grow, depends on δ.For nearly symmetric and distant planar models, the av-erages barely change between a pose and its alternatives in Sec. 4.2, and CF is large. Step 4 therefore compares these al-ternatives by EM on the individual observations. Sec. 5 tests MomentPnP in practice, and the appendix tests it beyond the theorems’ assumptions, with structured outliers (Fig. A14), partial views (Tab. A14) and large depth spreads (Tab. A19); proofs and further conditions in Apps. A.2 and A.6, further details in Apps. C.3 and C.7. 

## 5. Experiments 

Metrics. We report the median rotation error, the angle in degrees between estimated and true rotations [6], and the median relative translation error, the distance between the estimated and true translations divided by the true trans-lation length [44]. Accuracy is the percentage of frames with rotation error below 1◦, counting missing estimates as failures.

our own Step 3, the EM refinement of Sec. 4.2, run from ran-dom poses without Steps 1 and 2 and restarted until the time limit, so Tab. 1 also shows what refinement alone achieves. In the benchmark protocol [6], inliers are the released 2D–3D pairs, and outliers are drawn uniformly from the bounding box of each point set. We tuned the method on the test sets, so we also test on 1,000 held-out frames per dataset from the training split. Tab. 1 shows that no method has a lower median rotation error than MomentPnP on any test set. Compared with the best learned baseline, MomentPnP’s me-dian rotation error is 5 to 170 times smaller, and its median translation error is 9 to 320 times smaller. On the sampled test frames of Tab. 1, its accuracy is 97.3 to 100% without outliers and 82.3 to 97.0% with as many outliers as inliers. Averaged over the three test sets without outliers, 99.1% of frames are within 1◦, against 38.3% for the best learned baseline. The curve of MomentPnP lies above those of all learned baselines at every threshold (Fig. 5, Fig. A3). With half as many uniform outliers as inliers in each point set, MomentPnP reaches 95% accuracy on the 126 images with at most 2,000 inliers (Tab. A12). The best learned baseline reaches 33%. On all 169 images, without the inlier limit, MomentPnP reaches 87% and the best learned baseline 29% (Tab. A13). Fig. 4 shows real scenes (Fig. A7) and Fig. 6 frames of the point-only benchmarks. 

5.2. Large point sets and full scenes 

Scaling to large point sets. Beyond the benchmark sizes of up to about 12,000 points, more points reduce the random part of the noise in the moments but crowd the projections that each correspondence must separate. We test both effects on three ETH3D laser scans [65], each rendered into three views with one pixel of noise, ignoring occlusion. From 1,000 to 256,000 points, the median rotation error falls from 

0.002 ◦ to 0.00009 ◦ (Fig. 8), and at one million points it is 

0.00007 ◦ (Fig. 7, Tab. A14), in about 20 minutes per frame with GPU memory linear in the point count (Fig. A1). The learned baselines bring at most two of the nine poses within 

1◦ and return no pose from 64,000 points (Tab. A17). At one million points, 21 to 25% of the 2D points lie nearer to another projection than to the one that generated them, up from none at 1,000 points, so a nearest-projection labeling cannot resolve them (Fig. A11). The rotation error nevertheless falls over the same range, because moment 7Table 3. Global search on complete inputs. matching and EM use every point without requiring each correspondence to be correct. 

Full scene reconstructions. We also localize single im-ages against complete ETH3D scene reconstructions, where each query image observes an unknown part of the whole scene without a pose prior. MomentPnP recovers all 39 tuning queries within 1◦ and 35 of 39 held-out test queries (Tab. A18). On the tuning queries, the best learned baseline, Deep Blind PnP, recovers two. 

5.3. Ablation study and failure modes 

Role of moment matching. Three studies isolate what mo-ment matching contributes. (i) From the same initial poses on 100 test frames per benchmark, moments through order three or four bring 53 to 78 of these poses within 1◦ and 1% translation error before EM (Tab. A9). The mean and covariance bring at most two, because distinct orientations can share them. (ii) EM-ICP (ours), our EM refinement from random poses restarted until a 30-second limit, needs those 30 seconds per frame to reach the median errors that Mo-mentPnP reaches in at most 0.06 seconds without outliers (Tab. A4). With as many outliers as inliers, it reaches at most 31% accuracy (Tab. 1, Fig. A4). (iii) Given a domain that contains the true camera center and about six hours per search, GOPAC and GOSMA bring none of the thirty outlier-free frames within 1◦. MomentPnP needs no such domain and brings all thirty within 1◦, in about 2 seconds per frame (Tab. 3, Tab. A10). Controls on the depth spread, the image noise and the kind of outliers are in App. C.7. Under noise, the rotation error follows that of the pose fitted to the true correspondences, and with outliers on scene structure, every frame with up to 30% replaced stays within 1◦.

Independently selected inputs and failure modes. So far, every inlier 2D point is the image of a model point (Fig. 1), but 2D points detected without reference to the 3D model may have no corresponding model point. With SIFT keypoints [49] in the query image and a COLMAP scene model [64] built from other photographs, the search over 2D: SIFT detections 3D: reconstructed points estimated camera

camera poses still recovers Facade 26 within 1◦ (Fig. 9(a), Tab. A22). Such inputs can fail in two ways. In Meadow, only 255 of the 12,069 detections belong to reconstruction tracks, and many others lie on vegetation and sky absent from the model (Fig. 9(b), Tab. A23). A dense model poses a second difficulty, because projections can lie near most 2D points even at an incorrect pose. In another Facade image, 81% of the 2D points lie within 3.4 pixels of a projected laser-scan point at the reference pose, and 77% still do at a pose rotated by 2◦ (Fig. 9(c)); further details of every study in App. C. All code will be released. 

## 6. Conclusion 

In blind PnP, the camera pose need not wait for the corre-spondences. Finitely many feature averages of the two point sets determine it, and the correspondences then follow from nearest projections. The theory explains why this works on complete inputs and with uniform outliers, and the experi-ments show that it holds beyond these assumptions. Learned methods inherit the limits of their training data [63, 73], lose accuracy outside it and run out of memory on large point sets. Using neither training nor descriptors, this inference offers an alternative to both and to branch-and-bound search. 

Limitations. When 2D points have no corresponding model point, the feature averages need not match even at the true pose, so independently selected inputs remain harder than tracked ones. Adversarial outliers can prevent recovery. 

Future work. Missing structure and unequal sampling might be handled by reweighting the projections with unbal-anced optimal transport [10]. Moment matching may also extend to relative pose estimation and to monocular depth estimation of unknown scale [15, 80]. 8LLM Usage Statement 

We used OpenAI’s Codex to assist with editing and refining prose, code and implementation, figures, and formatting. No LLMs or AI tools were used to develop the research ideas, hypotheses, scientific claims, or conclusions. The authors take full responsibility for the accuracy and integrity of the paper and its accompanying artifacts.

12 Correspondence-Free Camera Pose Estimation by Matching Moments 

# Supplementary Material 

App. A gives the notation, method details and proofs, App. B the implementation, and App. C the experiments. Appendix figures, tables, equations and statements carry the prefix A, and unprefixed numbers refer to the main paper. 

## A. Method details and proofs 

Tab. A1 summarizes the notation of the main paper and the appendix. 

A.1. Moments & measurement error 

This section bounds how much image noise changes the moments of the observations and describes the effect of errors in the model points. In the bound, the terms that are linear in the noise have zero mean, and their contribution decreases as 1/√N when the point sets grow (Secs. 4.3 and 5). The second part concerns the assumption that the model points are exact (Sec. 3). We first illustrate what moments distinguish and fix the coordinates in which we measure them. 

A scalar example. The sets of real numbers {− 2, −1, 3}

and {2, 1, −3} are reflections of each other about zero. Both have mean zero and second moment 14 /3, so their first two moments do not distinguish them. Their third moments are 

6 and −6, because (−2) 3 + ( −1) 3 + 3 3 = 18 and the re-flection changes the sign of every cube. An average does not depend on the order of the points, so the third moment distinguishes the two sets without any correspondence be-tween their points. Moment matching uses the same property in the image, where the moments of orders three and four distinguish orientations whose projections have equal mean and covariance (Fig. 2). The moment of order g of a 2D point set has several distinct entries, one for each monomial of degree g, and we now fix how we index them. 

Moment coordinates. For a nonempty finite collection A

of points in a Euclidean space and a positive integer g, the tensor power a⊗g of a point a ∈ A contains all products of 

g coordinate components, including repeated entries. The moment tensor is their average,

Image noise. The bound below concerns complete inputs in pixel coordinates, before the map h is applied, under the measurement model of Eq. (1). Its first term shrinks as

14 The right-hand side depends on the pose and the model but not on σ. The lemma therefore compares the observed moments with the moments of the projections at the true pose, which can be computed without correspondences. 

Step 2. Expansion in the noise. For s = 0 , the noise van-ishes almost surely, so U = U⋆ and the two moments agree almost surely. We assume s > 0 from now on. We expand 

(u⋆i + wi)⊗g by choosing the noiseless position or the noise vector in each of its g factors. Subtracting (u⋆i )⊗g leaves the 2g − 1 products that contain at least one noise factor. Products with one noise factor have zero mean, so averag-ing over the N independent observations reduces their root mean square. Products with several noise factors can have a nonzero mean that averaging does not remove, but they are of order s2. We bound the two groups separately and add their root-mean-square bounds by Minkowski’s inequality. 

Step 3. One noise factor. For each of the g positions of the noise factor, the products are linear in wi, inde-pendent across i and of zero mean, with second moment 

∥u⋆i ∥2( g−1) E∥wi∥2 ≤ 2s2ρ2( g−1) . Independence and zero means make the cross terms vanish when we square their average over i. Each average therefore has root mean square at most ρg−1sp2/N , and the g positions contribute the first term of Eq. (A2). 

Step 4. Several noise factors. A product with a ≥ 2

noise factors has norm ∥u⋆i ∥g−a∥wi∥a ≤ ρg−a∥wi∥a. Be-cause ∥wi∥2/s 2 is exponentially distributed with mean two, 

E∥wi∥2a = 2 aa! s2a, so the product has root mean square at most ρg−a√2aa! sa. Minkowski’s inequality gives the same bound for its average over i, and there are  ga

 choices of positions for the a noise factors. Summing both groups gives

Errors in the model points. The lemma treats the model points as exact, as Eq. (1) does. Suppose now that model point yσ(i) is measured with an error vi of covariance Σ3D ,i 

and that the image error wi has covariance Σ2D ,i , with vi

and wi independent. To first order at the true pose, the difference between observation i and the projection of the measured model point is wi − Jproj ,i R⋆vi. Here Jproj ,i is the Jacobian of the pixel projection proj at the true model point xσ(i) in camera coordinates. Independence makes the cross-covariances vanish, so this difference has covariance 

Σlin ,i = Σ 2D ,i + Jproj ,i R⋆Σ3D ,i (R⋆)⊤J⊤

> proj ,i

. (A6) The 3D term depends on the viewing direction through 

Jproj ,i and is in general anisotropic. At a fixed viewing direction and a fixed covariance in model units, it scales with the inverse square of the depth. The model assumes independent errors and exact intrinsics, so it omits the corre-lations that shared calibration errors introduce. Because the expression is linearized, the exact projected error need not be Gaussian. 

A.2. Proof of Theorem 1 

We use calibrated image coordinates, in which a camera point x with x3 > 0 projects to proj cal (x) = x1:2 /x 3,and we identify a model Y = {yj }Nj=1 with the vector 

(y1, . . . , yN ) ∈ R3N . For a pose ξ = ( R, t) under which every model point has positive depth, the raw projected moments are 

PY (ξ) = 1

N

> N

X

> j=1

ϕ4

 proj cal (Ryj + t) ∈ R14 ,

the fourteen moments of orders one to four of the calibrated projections, without centering or normalization. 

Theorem 1 (restated). Fix a pose ξ⋆ and an integer N ≥

20 . There is a set Z ⊂ R3N of Lebesgue measure zero with the following property. Let Y /∈ Z be a model whose 

N points all have positive depth under ξ⋆, and let ξ be a pose under which all its points have positive depth. If 

PY (ξ) = PY (ξ⋆), then ξ = ξ⋆.

An invertible affine map of the image preserves the equal-ity of moments through order four in both directions, because composition with the map or its inverse takes every polyno-mial of degree at most four to another such polynomial. The restated theorem therefore also covers the moments in pixels and the moments after the map h of Sec. 4.1, which the main text compares. On complete noiseless inputs, it shows that the true pose is generically the only zero of L4 among the poses that keep all model points in front of the camera, so the minimum that Step 2 searches for is unique. The proof shows that every kind of alternative pose imposes on each model point an equation that a generic point does not satisfy. It then counts dimensions to show that the models admitting such a pose form a null set. After the proof, Corollary A1 turns uniqueness into a stability statement for the perspective moment fit. Uniqueness cannot hold for every model. A quarter-turn of a cube about the axis through the centers of two opposite 15 faces maps its eight corners onto themselves, so a camera pose and its composition with this rigid motion give the same image. The theorem shows that such symmetric models are exceptional. 

Coordinates. We fix the true pose ξ⋆ = ( R⋆, t⋆) before choosing the model and express the model points in its cam-era coordinates as xj = R⋆yj + t⋆. This invertible affine change of coordinates of R3N preserves sets of Lebesgue measure zero, so it suffices to prove the theorem for the con-figurations X = {xj }Nj=1 with all xj3 > 0. The true projec-tion is then proj cal (xj ), and a competing pose ξ = ( R, t)

gives 

proj Q, γ (xj ) = proj cal (Qxj + γ),

where Q = R(R⋆)⊤ ∈ SO(3) and γ = t − Qt⋆. The map 

ξ 7 → (Q, γ) is a bijection from the poses onto SO(3) × R3

that sends ξ⋆ to (I3, 0) . In the true camera coordinates, the competing camera center is −Q⊤γ, so the two cameras have the same center exactly when γ = 0 .

Moment differences as polynomials. Let P04 denote the fourteen-dimensional space of bivariate polynomials of de-gree at most four with zero constant coefficient. Each 

P ∈ P04 is the inner product κ · ϕ4 of its coefficient vector 

κ ∈ R14 with ϕ4. The linear combination of the fourteen moment differences with weights κ is therefore the average, over the points, of P (proj Q, γ (xj )) − P (proj cal (xj )) . The proof distinguishes alternative poses by whether the camera center moves and whether the optical axis tilts. For each kind, the next lemma bounds the dimension of the space of polynomials whose values cannot detect the change of pose. 

Lemma A2 (Polynomial features under a pinhole camera) .

Let Q ∈ SO(3) and γ ∈ R3, let u = ( u1, u 2) ∈ R2 be calibrated image coordinates, and let x3 > 0 be the depth along the true camera ray (x3u, x 3). (a) If γ̸ = 0 , the subspace 

KQ, γ = {P ∈ P04 : P (proj Q, γ (x3u1, x 3u2, x 3)) 

is independent of x3} (A7) 

has dimension at most four and equals {0} if γ3̸ = 0 . Here the composition is a rational function of (u, x 3), and inde-pendence means that its partial derivative with respect to x3

vanishes identically. (b) If γ = 0 and (Q31 , Q 32 )̸ = (0 , 0) ,then P (proj Q, γ (u, 1)) − P (u) is a nonconstant function of u for every nonzero P ∈ P04. (c) If γ = 0 and 

(Q31 , Q 32 ) = (0 , 0) , then proj Q, γ (u, 1) = Gu for some 

G ∈ O(2) . If Q̸ = I3, then G̸ = I2, and the invariant sub-space KG = {P ∈ P04 : P ◦ G = P } has dimension at most eight. For P / ∈ K G, the polynomial u 7 → P (Gu) − P (u) is nonconstant. Proof. Part (a). We write a = Q(u, 1) , so that 

Q(x3u, x 3) + γ = x3a + γ, and z(u, x 3) =proj Q, γ (x3u, x 3). With e3 = (0 , 0, 1) , we have x3a +

uc = γ1:2 /γ 3, the image of the true camera center in the competing camera. Differentiation gives 

∂x3 z = γ3

x3(x3a3 + γ3)

at every point z(u, x 3). This polynomial in z vanishes on an open set and hence identically. We expand P in homo-geneous terms about uc. By Euler’s identity, the operator 

(z − uc) · ∇ multiplies the term of degree k by k, so every term of positive degree is zero. Thus P is constant, and its zero constant coefficient gives P = 0 . If γ3 = 0 , then 

γ1:2 ̸ = 0 and

so the point moves along the fixed direction γ1:2 as x3 varies. For P ∈ K Q, γ , the chain rule gives γ1:2 · ∇ P (z) = 0 on the same open set, hence as a polynomial identity. Such a P is a polynomial of degree at most four in the linear coordinate perpendicular to γ1:2 , with zero constant coefficient, and these polynomials form a space of dimension four. 

Part (b). We put zQ(u) = ( Q(u, 1)) 3 = Q31 u1 +

Q32 u2 + Q33 , an affine polynomial that is nonconstant be-cause (Q31 , Q 32 )̸ = (0 , 0) . The competing camera sends the line zQ = 0 to infinity. A nonzero P ∈ P04 has a de-gree dP ≥ 1 and a nonzero leading homogeneous part PdP ,and its composition with proj Q, γ is a quotient with de-nominator zdP 

> Q

. On the line zQ = 0 , the numerator equals 

PdP (( Q(u, 1)) 1:2 ). Along this line, the two coordinates 

(Q(u, 1)) 1:2 trace an affine line not through the origin, be-cause otherwise the invertible matrix Q would map a vector 

(u, 1) to zero. A nonzero homogeneous polynomial cannot vanish on this entire line, because scalar multiples of the line contain an open subset of R2. The numerator is there-fore not divisible by the irreducible polynomial zQ, so the composition has a pole on the line zQ = 0 . Subtracting the polynomial P (u) cannot remove this pole, so the difference is not a polynomial and in particular is nonconstant. 

Part (c). The third row of Q is (0 , 0, Q 33 ), so orthog-onality gives Q33 = ±1 and Q13 = Q23 = 0 . Hence 

G = Q1:2 ,1:2 /Q 33 ∈ O(2) . If G = I2, then Q = Q33 I3,and det Q = 1 gives Q = I3. If G is a reflection, we choose orthonormal coordinates along its axis and perpendicular to it. The reflection then fixes the eight of the fourteen monomials whose exponent in the perpendicular coordi-nate is even and negates the other six. If G is a rotation 16 by an angle θ / ∈ 2πZ, it multiplies the complex monomial 

(u1 + i u2)a(u1 − iu2)b by ei( a−b)θ. A nonidentity rotation therefore changes both linear polynomials and the two cubic polynomials with a − b = ±1. A half-turn also changes the other two cubic polynomials, and every other rotation changes the two quadratic polynomials with a − b = ±2.Thus at most eight independent polynomials are unchanged by a nonidentity G. If P /∈ K G, then P (Gu) − P (u) is nonzero, and its zero constant coefficient makes it noncon-stant. 

Proof of Theorem 1. For X = {xj }Nj=1 , the difference be-tween the competing and true moment vectors is

ϕ4(proj Q, γ (xj )) 

− ϕ4(proj cal (xj )) .

(A8) We consider only configurations and competing poses that make all true and competing depths positive, so Φ is smooth on its domain. Its zeros are those of PY (ξ) − P Y (ξ⋆), ex-pressed in calibrated image and transformed model coordi-nates. A zero with (Q, γ)̸ = ( I3, 0) is an alternative pose. We write ∂X Φ for the 14 × 3N derivative of Φ with respect to the point coordinates. A coefficient vector κ ∈ R14 with 

κ⊤∂X Φ = 0 defines the polynomial P = κ · ϕ4. The av-erage of P ◦ proj Q, γ −P ◦ proj cal over the points then has zero derivative with respect to every point. We show that the configurations X that admit an alternative pose form a set of dimension less than 3N .

Dimension facts. We use standard facts about semialge-braic sets [2]. Sets described by finitely many polynomial equalities and inequalities, and projections of such sets, are semialgebraic. Projection does not increase dimension. A semialgebraic set has dimension at most d if each of its points has a neighborhood in which the set lies in a smooth submanifold of dimension d. A family has dimension at most its number of parameters plus the largest dimension of its solution set at fixed parameters. A nonzero polynomial in k variables has a zero set of dimension at most k − 1. A semialgebraic subset of R3N of dimension less than 3N has Lebesgue measure zero. In each of the three cases below, a high rank of ∂X Φ bounds the dimension directly through the implicit-function theorem. If the rank is low, Lemma A2 gives a coefficient vector κ whose polynomial detects the change of pose, and that polynomial yields one equation for every point. 

Case 1. Distinct camera centers. We suppose γ̸ = 0 ,so the competing pose has six parameters. At a solution with rank ∂X Φ ≥ 10 , ten of the equations have independent derivatives, so near this solution the solution set lies in a sub-manifold of dimension 3N +6 −10 < 3N . If the rank is less than ten, the coefficient vectors κ with κ⊤∂X Φ = 0 form a space of dimension greater than four. By Lemma A2(a), we can therefore choose such a κ whose polynomial P lies outside KQ, γ , and we normalize κ to the unit sphere S13 .We write xj = ( xj3u⋆j , x j3) with true projection u⋆j and depth xj3 > 0. Moving a point along its true camera ray leaves proj cal (xj ) unchanged, so the zero derivative of the average along this direction gives 

ddx 3

P (proj Q, γ (x3u⋆j , x 3)) 

> x3=xj3

= 0 , j = 1 , . . . , N. 

For fixed (Q, γ, κ), the left-hand side is a rational function of xj whose denominator is a product of powers of the true and competing depths. Because P /∈ K Q, γ , it is not identically zero, so its numerator is a nonzero polynomial in the three coordinates of xj . Each point therefore lies in a set of dimension at most two. The equations involve separate points, so their joint solution set has dimension at most 2N .The six pose parameters and thirteen parameters of κ give total dimension at most 2N + 19 , which is less than 3N

exactly when N ≥ 20 . This case sets the bound on N in Theorem 1. 

Case 2. Same center, tilted optical axis. We suppose γ =0 and (Q31 , Q 32 )̸ = (0 , 0) . These rotations form an open subset of SO(3) , of dimension three. When the derivative with respect to the point coordinates has full rank fourteen, the local dimension of the solution set is at most 3N +3 − 14 < 3N . Otherwise, we choose a unit vector κ with 

κ⊤∂X Φ = 0 . The ray coordinates (u⋆j , x j3) are smooth coordinates for positive depth, and proj Q, γ (xj3u⋆j , x j3) = proj Q, γ (u⋆j , 1) because the two cameras share their center. The derivatives with respect to the two image coordinates therefore give 

∇u⋆j

P (proj Q, γ (u⋆j , 1)) − P (u⋆j ) = 0 , j = 1 , . . . , N. 

By Lemma A2(b), the function in brackets is a nonconstant rational function of u⋆j , so at least one of its partial deriva-tives is a nonzero rational function. The numerator of that partial derivative is a nonzero polynomial in two variables, so the critical set has dimension at most one in u⋆j , and the free depth adds one dimension, which gives at most 2N

dimensions for the point set. Adding three rotation parame-ters and thirteen parameters of κ bounds the dimension by 

2N + 16 < 3N .

Case 3. Same center, aligned optical axis. We suppose 

γ = 0 and (Q31 , Q 32 ) = (0 , 0) , with Q̸ = I3. By the proof of Lemma A2(c), Q33 = ±1. A rotation with Q33 = −1

gives every point the competing depth −xj3 < 0 and is excluded. The rotations with Q33 = 1 form a family of dimension one. A rank of at least six bounds the local di-mension of the solution set by 3N +1 −6 < 3N . Otherwise, the coefficient vectors κ with κ⊤∂X Φ = 0 form a space of dimension greater than eight, so Lemma A2(c) gives a unit κ whose polynomial satisfies P / ∈ K G. The argument 17 of Case 2 requires each u⋆j to be a critical point of the non-constant polynomial u 7 → P (Gu) − P (u). Its critical set has dimension at most one, which again gives at most 2N

dimensions for the point set. The one rotation parameter and thirteen parameters of κ give total dimension at most 

2N + 14 < 3N .

Conclusion. All sets in these arguments are semialge-braic, because the rotation constraints, positive depths, co-efficient normalization and rank conditions are polynomial equalities or inequalities after denominators are cleared. The conditions P /∈ K Q, γ and P /∈ K G also have this form, because each requires at least one coefficient of the corre-sponding rational or polynomial identity to be nonzero. The local dimension bounds hold throughout the sets of high rank. The parameter counts of the three cases cover the remaining ranks. The finitely many unions and projections of the solution sets therefore have dimension less than 3N .For the fixed true pose, we obtain a null set Z of config-urations outside which no other pose with positive depths has the same moments through order four. This exceptional set is semialgebraic of dimension less than 3N , so it also lies in the zero set of a nonzero polynomial in the model coordinates. Every joint density on R3N , including one for dependent points, assigns probability zero to this set. 

Perspective stability. Theorem 1 concerns exact moments, whereas Step 2 fits moments that contain error. Uniqueness together with compactness bounds the pose error of the fit by the moment errors, through a modulus that the corollary defines but does not compute. This bound is the perspective counterpart of Eq. (A21), and it concerns the moment fit before EM refinement. We hold the image map h fixed throughout the comparison, even if it was computed from the observations. We write μY (ξ; h) and μU (h) for the averages of ϕ4 ◦ h over the projections at pose ξ and over the observations. Because h does not change which poses give equal moments, Theorem 1 provides the uniqueness that the corollary assumes, for a generic model of N ≥ 20 

points and any compact set of poses with positive depths that contains ξ⋆.

Corollary A1 (Stability of the perspective pose) . We fix a 3D model and an invertible affine image map h, and write M (ξ) = μY (ξ; h). Let Eξ be a compact set of poses that contains ξ⋆ and places every model point at posi-tive depth, and suppose that ξ⋆ is the unique solution of 

M (ξ) = M (ξ⋆) in Eξ . We fix a model length ℓY > 0 and use the pose distance 

dY (ξ, ξ ′)2 = ∥R − R′∥2 + ∥t − t′∥2/ℓ 2 

> Y

.

For aξ > 0 with {ξ ∈ E ξ : dY (ξ, ξ ⋆) ≥ aξ } nonempty, we define Γξ (aξ ) as the minimum of ∥M (ξ)−M (ξ⋆)∥ over that set. For an observed vector μU (h) and a fitted pose ˆξ ∈ E ξ ,the observation error is ϵobs = ∥μU (h) − M (ξ⋆)∥ and the fit residual is ϵres = ∥M ( ˆξ) − μU (h)∥. Then Γξ (aξ ) > 0,and 

ϵobs + ϵres < Γξ (aξ ) implies dY ( ˆξ, ξ ⋆) < a ξ . (A9) 

In particular, if the residual of the fitted pose exceeds the residual ϵobs of the true pose by at most ϵfit ≥ 0, so that 

ϵres ≤ ϵobs + ϵfit , then 2ϵobs + ϵfit < Γξ (aξ ) suffices. Proof. Positive depths make M continuous on Eξ . The poses of Eξ at distance at least aξ from ξ⋆ form a compact set, and uniqueness makes their moment difference strictly positive, so the minimum Γξ (aξ ) is positive. The triangle inequality gives ∥M ( ˆξ) − M (ξ⋆)∥ ≤ ϵres + ϵobs . If dY ( ˆξ, ξ ⋆) ≥ aξ ,the definition of Γξ (aξ ) gives ∥M ( ˆξ) − M (ξ⋆)∥ ≥ Γξ (aξ ),which contradicts ϵres + ϵobs < Γξ (aξ ). Substituting ϵres ≤

ϵobs + ϵfit gives the final assertion. On complete inputs, image noise enters through ϵobs ,which Lemma A1 bounds before the map h is applied. If the observations are images of only some of the model points, the difference between their average and the average over all projections also enters ϵobs . With outliers, moment matching replaces the monomials by bounded features, which limit the influence of the outliers on the averages but need not remove their bias (Lemmas A6 and A17). When Γξ (aξ ) is small, a small moment discrepancy can coexist with a pose error of at least aξ . A large fit residual can indicate a failed search. 

A.3. Orientation from normalized moments 

On complete inputs, Step 1 also places initial cameras at orientations fitted under an affine camera of rank two, such as scaled orthography. This section shows that these ori-entations have errors at most proportional to the moment errors. We center both point sets and scale each to identity covariance, giving ˜Y and ˜U. Their means and covariances then agree, and only the orientation remains unknown. Com-plete noiseless affine observations satisfy ˜U = O ˜Y for an orientation O ∈ V = {O ∈ R2×3 : OO ⊤ = I2}, the first two rows of a rotation [36]. We therefore determine the orientation from the cubic and quartic moments. Let the moment pair T ∈ R25 collect the 10 cubic and 15 quartic moments of ˜Y, and let FT (O) and TU collect those of the projected model O ˜Y and of ˜U. For an estimate ˆO of the true orientation O⋆, the fit residual ˆϵ = ∥F T ( ˆO) − TU ∥ measures how well the estimate fits the observed moments, and the observation error ϵ = ∥TU − F T (O⋆)∥ measures their noise. 

Theorem A1 (Orientation from normalized moments) . Let both point sets have positive definite covariance. For every moment pair T outside a set of zero volume in R25 , distinct orientations give distinct moments FT (O), and there is a constant ΓT > 0 such that for all O⋆, ˆO ∈ V ∥ ˆO − O⋆∥ ≤ (ˆ ϵ + ϵ)/ΓT .

18 A model of N ≥ 34 points drawn from a joint density has such a moment pair with probability one. 

Here ΓT is the greatest lower bound of the change of the projected moments per unit change of orientation. It is zero for symmetric models such as the cube (Lemma A5) and small for nearly symmetric ones (Corollary A2). The theorem bounds the orientation error linearly by the moment errors, with the factor 1/ΓT , before any correspondence is formed. Under perspective projection, the normalized obser-vations are in general not the image of the normalized model under any orientation, because the depths of the model points vary. The observation error ϵ then contains this departure from an affine camera together with the image noise. Step 1 therefore uses the affine orientations as starting values, and the perspective fit of Step 2 refines them with the indi-vidual depths. The uniqueness of the perspective moment fit bounds the pose error by the moment errors, through a modulus without an explicit form (Corollary A1). In the depth study of Tab. A19, the affine initial poses have median rotation errors of 9◦ to 161 ◦ before perspective fitting and EM, and the full solver is within 1◦ on 88 of 90 poses.

Separation constant. The constant of Theorem A1 is the greatest lower bound of the ratio between a change of the projected moments and the change of orientation that causes it, in the Frobenius norm, 

ΓT = inf  

> O,O ′∈V
> O̸=O′

∥F T (O) − F T (O′)∥∥O − O′∥ . (A11) Outside the exceptional set defined in the proof, the deriva-tive of FT in orientation also has rank three at every O ∈ V .Step 4 of the proof uses this to show ΓT > 0.

Outline of the proof. The proof has four parts. First, we write each moment tensor as a homogeneous polynomial, so that FT (O) becomes the restriction of two polynomials to the plane spanned by the rows of O. Second, Lemma A4 counts the independent linear conditions on T under which two distinct orientations have equal moments, and those under which the derivative of FT vanishes in a tangent di-rection. Third, the proof of Theorem A1 combines these counts with the dimensions of the families of orientation pairs and tangent directions. Steps 1 to 3 of that proof show that the exceptional moment pairs T form a set of dimension at most 24 in the 25 -dimensional space of moment pairs. Outside this set, compactness gives ΓT > 0 (Step 4), and the definition of ΓT gives the error bound (Step 5). Fourth, a separate proof shows that a model of N ≥ 34 points drawn from a joint density avoids the exceptional set with probabil-ity one. After the proof, Lemma A5 shows that ΓT depends 19 continuously on T and vanishes for symmetric models. To-gether, these two properties imply that nearly symmetric models have small ΓT (Corollary A2). 

Moments as forms. A symmetric tensor of order g on R3

is equivalent to a form of degree g, which is a polynomial in three variables whose monomials all have total degree g.We use forms because restricting a polynomial to a plane is simpler to analyze than contracting a tensor. We give each form the Frobenius norm of its symmetric tensor, and a pair of forms the direct-sum norm of the two tensors, as in Theo-rem A1. The value of ΓT depends on this choice, because the Frobenius norm weights each distinct entry mα by its multiplicity (App. A.1). Unweighted norms of the distinct polynomial coefficients need not give the same value. In this representation, the normalized model moment Mg ( ˜Y) is the form

Projected moments as restrictions. The two orthonormal rows of an orientation O ∈ V span a plane in R3. We write ν for a unit normal of this plane, so that Oν = 0 

and O⊤O = I3 − νν ⊤ is the orthogonal projection onto the plane ν⊥. For image coordinates u ∈ R2, we have 

u · O ˜yj = O⊤u · ˜yj . The projected moment of order g is therefore the restriction of Fg to the plane, written in image coordinates, 

(RO Fg )( u) = Fg (O⊤u), u ∈ R2. (A13) The cubic and quartic projected moments form the pair

RO maps Sg onto Hg , because a form f ∈ H g has the exten-sion ζ 7 → f (Oζ), whose restriction is f . Two orientations O

The following lemma computes this rank. It also computes the rank of the derivative of FT in a tangent direc-tion, which plays the same role for two orientations that are infinitely close. 

Lemma A4 (Rank of the moment difference of two orien-tations) . Let O and O′ be orientations with unit normals ν

and ν′, distinct in parts (a) and (b). 

(a) If ν′̸ = ±ν, let dL be a unit vector orthogonal to both normals, and put o = OdL and o′ = O′dL. The rank of 

∆O,O ′ is 9 if o′̸ = ±o. It is 7 if o′ = o, and 8 if o′ = −o.

(b) If ν′ = ±ν, then O′ = GO for an orthogonal matrix 

G̸ = I2. The rank of ∆O,O ′ is 4 if G is a reflection or the rotation by π. It is 6 if G is a rotation by ±π/ 2 or ±2π/ 3,and 8 for every other rotation G̸ = I2.

(c) Let R⊥ be the rotation of the plane by π/ 2. The tangent space of V at O consists of the matrices ˙O =

ωR ⊥O + qν ⊤ with ω ∈ R and q ∈ R2. The derivative of FT at O in the direction ˙O, as a linear map of T , has rank 9 if ω̸ = 0 ̸ = q. Its rank is 7 if ω = 0 ̸ = q, and 8 if 

q = 0 ̸ = ω.Proof. Each operator acts on the cubic and the quartic com-ponent of T separately. In all three parts, we therefore com-pute the rank for each degree g ∈ { 3, 4} and add the two ranks. 

Part (a). The two planes are distinct and meet in the line spanned by dL. We first determine which pairs

For a rotation G by an angle θ, complex coordinates make the action diagonal. In the coordinate z = u1 + i u2, the monomials zj ¯zg−j with 0 ≤ j ≤ g span the complex forms of degree g, and f 7 → f ◦ G⊤ multiplies them by e−i(2 j−g)θ .This operator is real, so its fixed subspaces over R and over 

C have the same dimension, namely the number of indices j

with (2 j − g)θ ∈ 2πZ. Since G̸ = I2, the angle θ is not a multiple of 2π. For g = 3 , the integers 2j − g are ±1 and 

±3, so this dimension is 2 if 3θ ∈ 2πZ and 0 otherwise. For 

g = 4 , the integers are 0, ±2 and ±4, so the dimension is 

1, plus 2 if 2θ ∈ 2πZ, plus 2 if 4θ ∈ 2πZ. For a reflection 

G, choosing coordinate axes along and perpendicular to its axis gives (u1, u 2) 7 → (u1, −u2). The invariant forms are then those even in u2, with dimensions 2 for g = 3 and 3 for 

g = 4 . Subtracting these dimensions from 4 + 5 gives the ranks of part (b).

Proof of Theorem A1. Steps 1 to 3 show that the moment pairs T for which FT fails to be one-to-one, or has a deriva-tive of rank less than three, form a set of dimension at most 

24 . The sets involved are semialgebraic, that is, built by finite unions and intersections from polynomial equations and inequalities. As in the proof of Theorem 1, we use three facts about their dimension [2]. First, a semialgebraic map does not increase dimension. Second, the dimension of its domain is at most the dimension of its image plus the largest dimension of a fiber, the set of points mapped to one point. Third, a semialgebraic set of smaller dimension than its am-bient Euclidean space has Lebesgue measure zero and lies in the zero set of a nonzero polynomial. 

Step 1. Two orientations with the same moments. Let Tinj 

be the set of T ∈ T with ∆O,O ′ T = 0 for some O̸ = O′, so that FT is one-to-one exactly when T / ∈ T inj . We divide the pairs of distinct orientations into the families of Tab. A2. On each family, the rank of ∆O,O ′ is constant by Lemma A4. We consider the triples (O, O ′, T ) with (O, O ′) in a family 

D and ∆O,O ′ T = 0 . The fiber of these triples over a fixed pair (O, O ′) is the kernel of ∆O,O ′ , of dimension 25 minus the rank, and the pairs contribute at most dim D further parameters. By the first two facts, the set of T that occur in these triples has dimension at most 

dim D + 25 − rank ∆ O,O ′ . (A19) The families are semialgebraic. We choose the normals as the cross products of the rows of O and of O′, which are polynomial in the entries. Then ν′ = ±ν exactly when 21 O′(I3 − O⊤O) = 0 . On the families with distinct planes, the cross product d× = ν × ν′ is a nonzero multiple of dL,so o′ = ±o exactly when O′d× = ±Od×. The dimensions of the families follow from dim V = 3 . All pairs form a set of dimension 6. A pair with o′ = ±o is determined, up to finitely many choices, by O, the unit vector o and the angle between the two planes about dL = O⊤o, which gives dimension 3 + 1 + 1 = 5 . A pair with ν′ = ±ν has the form 

(O, GO ), which gives dimension 3 when G is one of finitely many rotations and 4 when G ranges over all rotations. The reflection pairs (O, GO ) also have dimension 4,and Eq. (A19) would give 25 for them, so we remove one redundant parameter. Rotating the image coordinates of both orientations by the same planar rotation G1 preserves equal moments. Indeed, Eq. (A13) gives ∆G1O,G 1GO T =(∆ O,GO T ) ◦ G⊤ 

> 1

, and composition with G⊤ 

> 1

is invertible, so the two kernels agree. As the rotation G1 varies, the conjugate G1GG −11 runs over all reflections. It therefore suffices to consider the pairs (O, diag(1 , −1) O), a family of dimension 3 with kernels of dimension 25 −4. The largest bound in Tab. A2 is 24 , so dim Tinj ≤ 24 .

Step 2. An orientation with a degenerate derivative. Let 

Timm be the set of T for which, at some orientation, the derivative of FT vanishes in some nonzero tangent direc-tion ˙O. For T /∈ T imm , the derivative is injective on the three-dimensional tangent space at every orientation, that is, it has rank three. Step 4 needs this property, because two orientations that approach each other produce such a direction in the limit. The condition does not change when 

˙O is multiplied by a nonzero scalar, so only the direction of 

˙O contributes parameters. The families of orientations and tangent directions have dimension 3 + 2 when ω̸ = 0 ̸ = q,dimension 3 + 1 when ω = 0 , and dimension 3 when q = 0 .By Lemma A4(c), the corresponding derivatives have ranks 

9, 7 and 8. The count of Eq. (A19) gives 21 , 22 and 20 , so 

dim Timm ≤ 22 .

Step 3. A set of measure zero. The exceptional set Texc =

Tinj ∪ T imm is semialgebraic of dimension at most 24 in the 

25 -dimensional space T . By the third fact, it has Lebesgue measure zero and lies in a proper algebraic subset of T . For 

T / ∈ T exc , the map FT is one-to-one on V and its derivative has rank three at every orientation. The first property is the first claim of Theorem A1, and Step 4 uses the second. 

Step 4. The lower bound is positive. Injectivity alone does not give ΓT > 0, because the ratio in Eq. (A11) could tend to zero along pairs of distinct orientations that approach each other. Compactness of V and Step 2 exclude this. We fix 

T / ∈ T exc and suppose that ΓT = 0 . There are then distinct orientations Oi, O ′

Proof of Theorem A1 for sampled models. Step 3 of the proof above shows that Texc has measure zero among mo-ment pairs. A model, however, is a point of R3N , and a set of measure zero among moment pairs need not have a preimage of measure zero among models. We therefore show that the map from point coordinates to T is a submersion at almost every point set, meaning that its derivative maps onto the space of moment pairs. The pair T depends on the point set through its 34 power sums, one sum over j for each mono-mial of degree one to four in the three coordinates, and we treat the map to the power sums and the map from them to 

T in turn. 

Step 1. From the point set to its power sums. The Jacobian of the power-sum map has one row per monomial, and the row of a monomial lists its gradients at y1, . . . , yN . These rows are linearly dependent exactly when a nonzero linear combination of the 34 monomials has zero gradient at every 

yj . Such a combination is a nonconstant polynomial, so the set where its gradient vanishes is a proper algebraic subset of R3, of dimension at most two. Up to scale, these combinations form a family of dimension 33 . Allowing each of the N points to lie in the corresponding set gives a semialgebraic set of dimension at most 33 + 2 N , which contains every point set at which the Jacobian loses rank. This set has measure zero in R3N because 33 + 2 N < 3N .22 Step 2. From the power sums to T . The derivative of the map from power sums to moment pairs has full row rank wherever the covariance is positive definite. With the sums of degrees one and two held fixed, normalization is a fixed invertible affine map of R3, and (F3, F 4) is an invertible affine function of the sums of degrees three and four. 

Step 3. Conclusion. For N ≥ 34 , the covariance is positive definite with probability one. By Steps 1 and 2, the map to T is a submersion on an open set whose complement has measure zero. After smooth changes of coordinates, a submersion acts locally as a coordinate projection, so the preimage of the null set Texc is null [43]. The distribution of the point set has a density on R3N , so it assigns probability zero to this preimage. Corollary A2 uses two further properties of ΓT , which the next lemma proves. 

Lemma A5 (Continuity of the lower bound and symmetric models) . There is a constant C such that |ΓT − ΓT ′ | ≤ 

C∥T − T ′∥ for all moment pairs T and T ′. If T = ( F3, F 4)

satisfies F3 ◦ Qs = F3 and F4 ◦ Qs = F4 for an orthogonal matrix Qs̸ = I3, then ΓT = 0 and T ∈ T exc .Proof. Continuity. The map FT (O) is linear in T and poly-nomial in O, and V is bounded. There is therefore a con-stant C such that ∥F T (O) − F T (O′)∥ ≤ C∥T ∥∥ O − O′∥

for all orientations and all T . Applying this bound to 

T − T ′, and the triangle inequality to the definition of ΓT ,gives |ΓT − ΓT ′ | ≤ C∥T − T ′∥. In particular, the set 

{T : Γ T > 0} is open.

Suppose that an invertible matrix L̸ = I3 and a permuta-tion π satisfy L(yj − ¯y) = yπ(j) − ¯y for every j. A mir-ror plane or a nontrivial rotational symmetry of the model has this form, because an isometry that maps a finite set to itself fixes its centroid. A.4. Near-symmetry & bounded features 

This section proves two facts that the main text uses. Corol-lary A2 shows that ΓT is small when the moment pair of a model is close to that of a symmetric model, so the bound of Eq. (A21) is weak for nearly symmetric models. Lemma A6 proves that, at the true pose, the expected feature averages over the observations and over the projections differ by 

η(μU,0−μY,0), the term that moment matching with outliers subtracts in Definition 1. 

Near-symmetry. A model with a symmetry has two distinct orientations with the same projected moments, so its moment pair lies in the exceptional set of Theorem A1 (Lemma A5). The next corollary shows that moment pairs near such a pair have a small constant ΓT , and hence weak orientation bounds. 

Corollary A2 (Near-symmetric models) . Let T0 be a mo-ment pair for which two distinct orientations give identical projected moments. Then ΓT0 = 0 , and every moment pair T

satisfies ΓT ≤ C∥T −T0∥ with the constant C of Lemma A5. Proof. At T0, the two orientations give a zero numerator in the definition of ΓT0 in Eq. (A11), so ΓT0 = 0 . Lemma A5 shows that |ΓT − ΓT ′ | ≤ C∥T − T ′∥ for all moment pairs 

T and T ′, and the choice T ′ = T0 gives the bound. 

Bounded features with outliers. Moment matching with outliers (Definition 1) uses the fact that, at the true pose, the inliers contribute equally to the average over the observations and to the average over the projections, so the two averages differ through the outliers alone. The next lemma states 23 this identity for the feature means of two distributions. It also bounds the change of a feature average when a fraction of the points is replaced. Without a bound on the features, one replaced point could change the average arbitrarily, so moment matching uses bounded features when outliers may be present. 

Lemma A6 (Bounded features with outliers) . Let ϕ : R2 →

Rdϕ be a measurable feature map with sup u∈R2 ∥ϕ(u)∥ ≤ 

1. Replacing a fraction 0 ≤ η ≤ 1 of the points of a nonempty finite collection of 2D points by other 2D points changes the average of ϕ over the collection by at most 2η.Consider two distributions on R2, each a mixture of an inlier component with weight 1 − η and an outlier component with weight η. Let the two inlier components have the same fea-ture mean ϕin , and let the outlier components have feature means μU,0 and μY,0. Then the feature means μ1 and μ2

of the two distributions satisfy 

μ1 = (1 − η)ϕin + ημU,0,

μ2 = (1 − η)ϕin + ημY,0,

μ1 − μ2 = η(μU,0 − μY,0).

(A23) 

Proof. Each replaced point changes the feature sum by a vector of norm at most two, so division by the size of the collection gives 2η. Each mixture mean is the weighted average of its inlier and outlier means, and subtracting the two cancels the common inlier term. If both point sets undergo replacements, the difference of their feature averages changes by at most twice the sum of the replaced fractions. These bounds hold for a fixed feature map and a fixed image normalization, and they do not shrink as the number of points grows. Lemma A17 gives a bound that does shrink when the outlier model points have the projected distribution of the inliers. 

A.5. Poisson reference for the association level 

Definition 2 compares the distance from each observation to its nearest projection with the local spacing of the projec-tions. This section proves that, if the projections around an observation form a homogeneous Poisson process indepen-dent of it, the statistic Ei has a unit exponential distribution at every intensity. The association level compares the ob-served statistics with this reference, as App. B.1 specifies. 

Lemma A7 (Exponential nearest-neighbor statistic) . Let a 2D point be chosen independently of a homogeneous Poisson process of positive intensity on R2. For an integer ℓ ≥ 1, let 

d1 ≤ · · · ≤ dℓ+1 be the distances from this point to its ℓ + 1 

nearest points of the process. Then 

E = −ℓ log(1 − d21/d 2

> ℓ+1

) ∼ Exp(1) . (A24) 

Proof. Conditional on dℓ+1 , the ℓ nearer points of a homo-geneous Poisson process are independent and uniform in the disk of radius dℓ+1 about the fixed point. The fraction of the area of that disk within distance d of its center is d2/d 2

> ℓ+1

,so the ratios d2 

> j

/d 2 

> ℓ+1

for 1 ≤ j ≤ ℓ are the order statis-tics of ℓ independent uniform random variables on [0 , 1] ,whatever the intensity. For x ≥ 0, their minimum exceeds 

1 − e−x/ℓ with probability (e−x/ℓ )ℓ = e−x, and this event is {E > x }.The lemma concerns one point chosen independently of the Poisson process. In MomentPnP, we estimate the pose from the observations, and the neighborhoods of different observations can share projections. Sums of these statis-tics therefore need not follow a Gamma distribution, and App. B.1 compares the association level with fixed thresh-olds. 

A.6. Finite Fourier matching with uniform outliers

We now show that, under its mixture model with uni-form outliers, finitely many of the method’s Fourier features determine the pose and the outlier fraction, and we bound the error caused by finitely many sampled outliers. The results concern the objective of Definition 1, before EM refinement. We first fix the setting and restate Theorem 2 in its notation.

The atomic parts of the two sides therefore coincide. After division by (1 − η⋆)/m > 0, the atomic part on the left puts mass WF (pj (ξ)) at each pj (ξ),with the masses of coinciding points added. The left side has at most m points of positive mass, so the pj (ξ) are distinct, each lies where WF is pos-itive, and they are the points z⋆j . Thus P in

The derivative of rK1 at θ⋆ is then injective, with least singular value σ1 > 0 in the coordinates (ω, τ , η ).

Rank condition. The rank condition on the projections is generic. At the identity camera, with u = x1:2 /x 3 for a camera point x, the derivatives with respect to (ω, τ ) are 

∂u 1 = ( −u1u2, 1 + u21, −u2, x −13 , 0, −u1/x 3),∂u 2 = ( −(1 + u22), u 1u2, u 1, 0, x −13 , −u2/x 3).

For the camera points (0 , 0, 1) , (2 , 0, 2) and (0 , 3, 3) , the six rows form a matrix with determinant 29 /36 . The 6×6 minor formed by the rows of three points is a rational function of their coordinates whose denominator is a power of their positive depths. Its numerator is a nonzero polynomial, so it vanishes only on a set of measure zero in R9. Rank less than six forces this minor to vanish, so the configurations of m ≥ 3 points with rank less than six form a null set. Expressing the points in the camera coordinates of ξ⋆ is an invertible affine change of the model coordinates, and the box normalization multiplies each ∂pj by the same invertible matrix. The exceptional set of inlier models is therefore null as well.

Relation to the fitted fraction. The method sets the frac-tion at each pose to the least-squares value ˆη(ξ) of Step 2 in App. B, clipped to [0 , 0.75] . The theorems cover this choice with I = [0 , 0.75] and ηmax = 0.75 whenever

Pose changes therefore remain distinguishable after the fraction is fitted, and the squared norm of the profiled residual grows at least quadratically in the pose error near ξ⋆. If, in addition, rpr is continuously differentiable with a Lipschitz derivative near 

ξ⋆, the local convergence theory of Gauss–Newton for zero-residual problems with an injective derivative applies [57]. Theorem A3 bounds the error of every returned parameter by its residual, so it does not require the search to attain the global minimum. 

Scope. The theorems hold for each fixed set of weights, that is, for each fixed spectral width ζF . The schedule of 28 the method pairs each cutoff with its own spectral width (Tab. A3), so the theorems apply to each stage of the schedule separately. The error terms of Eq. (A27) account for finite samples and the grid approximation. 

A.7. Ambiguous poses 

Step 4 refines seven alternatives to the accepted pose, ob-tained by half-turns about the principal axes of the model points and about the optical axis (Sec. 4.2). This section shows why these are the poses to test. Under scaled or-thography, the accepted pose and its seven alternatives give images with the same mean and covariance for every model (Lemma A8(a)). An alternative gives the same scaled-orthographic image as the accepted pose when the model has the corresponding symmetry (part (b)). For a planar model, the alternative that mirrors the depths about the centroid does so (part (c)). A final bound shows that, under perspective projection, mirroring the depths moves each image point by at most an amount that grows with the depth variation of the model, so the two images stay close for shallow models. 

Setting. We use calibrated image coordinates, with K = I3,and state the results for a 3D model Y. Step 4 applies them to all model points on complete inputs and to the model points matched at the accepted pose when outliers may be present. Let ¯y and ΣY be the centroid and covariance of the model points, and let e1, e2, e3 be an orthonormal basis of eigen-vectors of ΣY , the principal axes. The half-turn about the 

kth principal axis is Hk = 2 eke⊤

− I3 for k = 1 , 2, 3,and H0 = I3. The half-turn about the optical axis is 

D = diag( −1, −1, 1) , and −D = diag(1 , 1, −1) reverses depth. A pose (R, t) maps yj to xj = Ryj +t in camera co-ordinates and the centroid to ¯x = R ¯y+t, with positive depth 

¯x3. The scaled-orthographic image of xj is (xj1, x j2)/¯x3,and its pinhole image is proj cal (xj ) = ( xj1, x j2)/x j3 when 

xj3 > 0. The eight poses of Step 4 are the accepted pose and its seven alternatives. For k = 0 , . . . , 3, they are
