A.8. Matching under an accurate pose 

At the selected pose, MomentPnP labels each observation with its nearest projection. We bound the probability that any of these labels is wrong when every projection is predicted within a tolerance ϵpred . The bound grows with the den-sity of the projections and with the number of points, which makes precise the remark of Sec. 5 that each correspondence depends on the spacing between nearby projections. It tends to zero as N s and N ϵ pred tend to zero, and App. A.9 com-pares this with the noise that a joint search over the camera and the correspondences tolerates. 

Setting. We consider complete inputs with n = N ≥ 2

in which every model point lies at positive depth at the true pose ξ⋆. We number the observations so that the true correspondence is the identity. Then ui = pi + wi with 

pi = pi(ξ⋆). We assume that the projections pi ∈ R2 are independent, each with a density bounded by fmax , that they are jointly independent of the errors w1, . . . , wN , and that E∥wi∥2 = 2 s2. The bound uses no other property of the projection, so it holds for any camera model. A pre-diction ˆpj of pj is any measurable function of the data. For an estimated pose ( ˆR, ˆt) that places every model point at positive depth, the prediction is ˆpj = proj( ˆRyj + ˆ t).The predictions have maximum error ˆδ = max j ∥ ˆpj − pj ∥.Nearest-projection matching assigns to observation i a label 

ˆσ(i) that minimizes ∥ui − ˆpj ∥ over j, with ties resolved by a fixed measurable rule. 

Lemma A9 (Matching error under an accurate pose) . Under the assumptions above, every fixed tolerance ϵpred ≥ 0 on the prediction error satisfies 

Pr ˆσ̸ = id and ˆδ ≤ ϵpred 



≤ 8πf max N (N − 1)(2 s2 + ϵ2pred ). (A36) 

Proof. We show that an observation can receive a wrong label only if another projection lies close to its own. We then bound the probability of such a close pair by the density bound. 

Step 1. A well-separated observation is labeled cor-rectly. We put di = min j̸ =i∥pi − pj ∥, and let Ωi = {di >

2( ∥wi∥ + ϵpred )} be the event that observation i is well separated. On Ωi ∩ { ˆδ ≤ ϵpred }, every j̸ = i satisfies 

∥ui − ˆpj ∥ ≥ ∥ pi − pj ∥ − ∥ wi∥ − ∥ ˆpj − pj ∥≥ di − ∥ wi∥ − ϵpred 

> ∥wi∥ + ϵpred ≥ ∥ ui − ˆpi∥.

(A37) The first line is the triangle inequality, and the last uses 

ui − ˆpi = wi + pi − ˆpi. Observation i is therefore strictly closest to prediction i, and ˆσ(i) = i whatever the tie rule. Hence a wrong label with ˆδ ≤ ϵpred requires at least one Ωi

to fail. 

Step 2. Probability of a close pair. We condition on pi

and wi, since the other projections are independent of them. A disk of radius d has area πd 2, so by the density bound each other projection falls in the disk of radius d about pi with probability at most πf max d2. Taking d = 2( ∥wi∥ + ϵpred )

and using (∥wi∥ + ϵpred )2 ≤ 2∥wi∥2 + 2 ϵ2pred , we obtain 

Pr[Ω ci ] ≤ (N − 1) πf max E4( ∥wi∥ + ϵpred )2

≤ 8πf max (N − 1)(2 s2 + ϵ2pred ). (A38) A union bound over the N observations gives Eq. (A36). At the true pose, the predictions equal the projections, so ˆδ = 0 

and Step 2 applies with ϵpred = 0 and E[4 ∥wi∥2] = 8 s2.Nearest-projection matching then errs with probability at most 8πf max N (N − 1) s2.

A.9. Exact affine matching threshold 

Shuffled regression estimates a linear map without known input-output pairings [75], and moment-based methods re-cover this map by comparing averages [47, 74]. Exact re-covery of every pairing in shuffled regression [50, 81] and geometric planted matching [41, 67] requires the noise to 30 stay below a threshold. We examine when every corre-spondence can be recovered. We compare estimating the correspondences after estimating the camera with a joint search over both. MomentPnP estimates the pose first and then labels each observation with its nearest projection (Sec. 4.2). Theo-rem A4 concerns a joint least-squares search over an affine camera and all permutations of the model points, which dif-fers from the perspective fitting and EM of MomentPnP. For random model points, it shows that this search recovers every correspondence with probability tending to one when 

N s → 0, and that no estimator does so when N s does not tend to zero, even with the camera known. For fixed noise 

s > 0, exact recovery of the correspondences therefore fails as the point sets grow. Lemma A9 gives the correspond-ing statement for nearest-projection matching. Under its assumptions, which do not depend on the camera model, nearest-projection matching recovers every label with prob-ability tending to one if N s → 0 and if the largest error ˆδ

of the predicted projections satisfies Pr[ ˆδ ≤ ϵpred ] → 1 for tolerances ϵpred with N ϵ pred → 0. Given an accurate pose, labeling after pose estimation therefore tolerates noise of the same order as the joint search. Under the random model of Theorem A4, no estimator tolerates noise of a larger order. 

Theorem A4 (Exact affine matching threshold) . Fix a dis-tribution on R3 that either has a bounded density on a bounded set or is a nondegenerate Gaussian, and fix an affine camera y 7 → Ay + b with A ∈ R2×3 of rank two and 

b ∈ R2. Draw N model points y1, . . . , yN independently from this distribution, and observe ui = Ayσ(i) + b + wi

for i = 1 , . . . , N and an unknown permutation σ. The noise vectors wi ∼ N (0 , s 2I2) are independent of each other and of the model. The noise level s = sN ≥ 0 may depend on 

N as N → ∞ . If N s → 0, the correspondence that min-imizes the least-squares reprojection error jointly with the unknown camera is σ with probability tending to one. In the bounded case, once N s is below a constant, this probability is at least 1 − Cth (N 2s2 + e−cth N ), where the constants 

Cth , c th > 0 depend on the distribution and the camera. If 

lim sup N s > 0, no estimator recovers a uniformly random 

σ with probability tending to one, even when the camera is known. 

Scope. If the projections have unit variance per coordinate, the signal-to-noise ratio is SNR = 1 /s 2. The threshold in Theorem A4 then lies at SNR of order N 2, and exact recov-ery requires SNR /N 2 → ∞ . The theorem assumes a fixed model dimension, two image coordinates, Gaussian noise and outlier-free point sets under an affine camera. It con-cerns the event that every label is correct, whose probability Fig. A15 measures. One wrong label makes this event fail. Fig. A11 measures the percentage of wrong labels, which grows with N because each label depends on the density of the projections near it (Lemma A9). Over the same range of point counts, pose accuracy keeps improving up to one million points (Fig. 8). This behavior is consistent with mo-ments averaging over all points, and it motivates estimating the pose before the correspondences. 

Outline of the proof. Least squares selects the true corre-spondence when every incorrect correspondence fits worse after the noise is added. We therefore first show that, with-out noise, every incorrect correspondence leaves a loss that no affine camera removes, and then that noise rarely can-cels this loss. The argument depends on how many points the correspondence moves. When it moves few points, its loss is at least half its total squared displacement, because each point has little influence on the fitted camera (Lem-mas A10 and A11). When it moves many points, its loss exceeds a fixed constant, because a fixed affine map rarely fits it. A count of permutations and a finite covering of the affine maps extend this bound to all maps at once (Lem-mas A12 to A14). Lemma A15 combines the two cases, and Lemma A16 bounds the probability that noise cancels the loss of one correspondence. Theorems A5 and A6 treat bounded and Gaussian model points. Each averages these probabilities over the model points and sums them over all correspondences, using the second bound of Lemma A12 for correspondences that move few points. Theorem A7 proves the converse from a close pair of projections that noise of order 1/N confuses. The proof of Theorem A4 combines the three theorems. 

Setting. The proof allows model points in Rq for any di-mension q ≥ 2, with q = 3 in Theorem A4. For N ≥ 2, the model points y1, . . . , yN ∈ Rq are independent and identi-cally distributed. The camera is an affine map (A, b) with 

A ∈ R2×q of rank two and b ∈ R2, fixed independently of the sample. Scaled orthography is the case in which A

is a positive multiple of a matrix with orthonormal rows. The dimension, the distribution and the camera stay fixed as N grows. The true projections are pi = Ayi + b. We relabel the observations so that the true correspondence is the identity id , which gives 

ui = pi + wi, wi ∼ N (0 , s 2I2), (A39) with noise vectors that are independent of each other and of the model. From here on, σ denotes any candidate corre-spondence, that is, any permutation of {1, . . . , N }.

Definition A1 (Bounded model) . The model is bounded if (i) ∥yi∥ ≤ Ymax almost surely, (ii) the covariance of yi is positive definite and (iii) pi has a density on R2 bounded by 

fmax .The bounded distributions of Theorem A4 satisfy these conditions. If yi has a density bounded by a constant and supported in the ball of radius Ymax , then (i) and (ii) hold. Condition (iii) then holds with fmax the product of the bound on the density of yi, the volume of the unit ball in Rq−2 and 

Y q−2max / det( AA ⊤)1/2. A sample from a surface need not satisfy (iii), because its projection can have an unbounded density at the contour. In the bounds below, all constants depend on the distribution and on A.

Proof. We write εi = pi − A′yσ(i) − b′ for the residual of observation i. A moved model point enters two residuals, so the residuals are not independent. We therefore drop one residual from each cycle of length at least two and integrate the remaining r residuals one at a time, each against a model point that no later residual involves. 

Step 1. Choice of residuals. We write a cycle of σ

of length at least two as i0 → i1 → · · · → i0, with

Theorem A7 (No exact recovery above the noise threshold) .

Suppose that the true projections p1, . . . , pN are indepen-dent with a common density fp on R2 bounded by fmax ,and let Ip = R f 2p be the squared L2 norm of this density. The observations are ui = pσ(i) + wi, where σ is the true correspondence and the noise vectors wi ∼ N (0 , s 2I2) are independent of each other and of the model. Suppose that σ

is uniformly distributed over all permutations and indepen-dent of the model and the noise, and let the estimator know the model and the camera (A, b). For every c0 > 0 there is an N0(fp, c 0) such that, whenever N ≥ N0 and N s ≥ c0,every measurable estimator errs with probability at least 

Φ −1/√2 · (πc 20Ip/8) 2

πf max c20/2 + 14 π2f 2max c40

> 0, (A64) 

where Φ is the standard normal distribution function. The same bound holds for the worst case over the true corre-spondence. Proof. With probability bounded away from zero, two true projections lie within c0/N of each other. At noise level 

s ≥ c0/N , an estimator confuses this pair with positive probability even when it knows all other labels. 

Step 1. A close pair occurs with probability bounded away from zero. Let Nclose be the number of unordered pairs with ∥pi − pj ∥ ≤ c0/N . For d > 0, let fp,d (ud) be the average of fp over the disk of radius d about ud, so that 

fp,d ≤ fmax and Pr[ ∥p1 − p2∥ ≤ d] = πd 2 R fpfp,d . By the Lebesgue differentiation theorem [61], fp,d → fp almost everywhere as d → 0. Since fpfp,d ≤ fmax fp, dominated convergence gives R fpfp,d → Ip > 0. For N ≥ N0,therefore,

(A65) Two distinct pairs are both close with probability at most 

(πf max c20/N 2)2. For disjoint pairs this follows from in-dependence, and for pairs that share a point it follows by conditioning on that point. There are fewer than 

N 4/4 ordered choices of two distinct pairs, so EN 2close ≤

ENclose + 14 π2f 2max c40. By the second-moment method, which is the Cauchy–Schwarz inequality, Pr[ Nclose > 0] ≥

(ENclose )2/EN 2close . This is at least the fraction that multi-plies Φ( −1/√2) in Eq. (A64). 

Step 2. A close pair forces a positive error probability. 

We choose {i, j } as a closest pair of true projections, with the choice determined by the model. We reveal to the esti-mator the true correspondence of every other model point, together with the unordered pair of observations generated by pi and pj . Given the revealed labels, the two remain-ing assignments have equal prior probabilities. Under each assignment, the ordered pair of observations is Gaussian in 

R4 with covariance s2I4. The two means are distinct almost surely and lie √2 dmin apart. With equal priors and a com-mon isotropic covariance, the nearest-mean test is optimal. Projection onto the line through the means gives its error probability Φ( −dmin /(√2s)) , which is at least Φ( −1/√2) 

when dmin ≤ c0/N ≤ s, that is, on {Nclose > 0}. An estimator without the revealed information cannot do better, so it errs with at least this probability on {Nclose > 0}. Inte-grating over the model gives Eq. (A64). The worst-case error over the true correspondence is at least the average. 36 Proof of Theorem A4. We combine the three preceding the-orems for a fixed distribution and camera, using the con-stants of each. For 0 < s 2 ≤ c1/N 2, the probability that the least-squares correspondence is wrong is at most 

Cth (N 2s2 + e−cth N ) for a bounded model by Theorem A5, and at most Cth (N 2s2 + 1 /N ) for Gaussian model points by Theorem A6. For s = 0 , the loss L equals L0, which vanishes at the identity. When dmin > 0, which holds almost surely, every σ̸ = id has positive noiseless loss on Ωsep for a bounded model (Lemma A15) and on Ωsep ,N for Gaussian model points (proof of Theorem A6). The error probability is then at most Cdes e−cth N or CG/N , respectively. Since the proof of Theorem A5 takes Cth ≥ Cdes , the bounded case gives the stated bound whenever N s ≤ √c1. If N s → 0,then eventually s2 ≤ c1/N 2, and all these bounds tend to zero. If lim sup N s > 0, Theorem A7 applies along a subse-quence in both models, so no estimator recovers a uniformly distributed correspondence with probability tending to one, even when the camera is known. If ties among minimiz-ing correspondences are broken uniformly, relabeling the input relabels the output distribution in the same way. The error probability of least squares is then the same for every true correspondence, so it does not recover any fixed true correspondence with probability tending to one either. 

A.10. Outliers on the structure of the inliers 

Moment matching models outliers as uniform. Real outliers often lie on the same structure as the inliers (Sec. 5), and we treat this case for the model points. Suppose that the outlier model points project with the same image distribution as the inliers. Including them then changes the feature average of the projections by at most 1/√m in root mean square, which is small when the number m of inliers is large. App. C.7 tests the method when outliers among the 2D points lie on the scene structure. The assumption concerns the distribution of the projec-tions in the image. Model points on the same physical sur-face need not have a common projected distribution, for example when visibility or sampling density differs between them.

Outliers from another distribution. Without the common distribution, Eq. (A68) and ∥ϕ∥ ≤ 1 give the deterministic bound 2Nout /N , as in Lemma A6. For a fixed fraction 

Nout /N of outliers, this bound does not decrease as the number of points grows. Suppose that the projections are independent, with the inliers drawn from one distribution with feature mean μ and the outliers from another with feature mean μout . The computation of Steps 2 and 3 then shows that the difference in the lemma lies within 1/√m of 

(Nout /N )( μout −μ) in root mean square. For a fixed outlier fraction and μout ̸ = μ, this vector is nonzero and does not change as the point sets grow. Differences in visibility or sampling density between the inlier and the outlier model points can produce this case, even when the outliers lie on the same structure as the inliers. 

A.11. Camera initialization 

Camera pose can be estimated by aligning point distribu-tions [5]. We initialize the camera position and orientation from observations U, a 3D model Y and known intrinsics K.Model points outside the observed view may have no image counterparts. We search for poses whose projections concen-trate near the observations and select the model points that project into the observed image region. These points form 37 the model for refining the pose by expectation–maximization (EM) and for the alignment check. 

Camera centers and orientations. We parameterize a pose by its rotation R and camera center c, with t = −Rc. We form a box from the 1st and 99th percentiles of the model coordinates along the principal axes of the model. The di-agonal of this box defines the scene extent, and we enlarge the box by half of each side length on both sides. Three full grids cover the enlarged box with maximum cell side lengths of 20%, 10% and 5% of the scene extent. Along a box axis of length Lk, a level with cell-size limit ∆c has ⌈Lk/∆c⌉

intervals of equal length. We test the center of every result-ing 3D cell. At each center, we test 11,520 rotations, using 640 viewing directions distributed over the sphere and 18 roll angles separated by 20 ◦. The box bounds the initial grid, but local refinement can move the center beyond it. 

Projection densities. The calibrated observation ucal ,i con-sists of the first two coordinates of K−1(u⊤ 

> i

, 1) ⊤. At a pose ξ = ( R, t), let pcal ,j (ξ) be the first two coordinates of 

R(yj − c) divided by its depth. We use a narrow and a broad Gaussian bandwidth 0 < τ n < τ b. Let Jcam (ξ) contain the indices of model points at positive depth whose projections lie in the bounding rectangle of the calibrated observations, extended by 4.25 τb on each side. For a bandwidth τ > 0,the smoothed projection density is 

fτ (z; ξ) = X

> j∈J cam (ξ)

exp   − ∥ z − pcal ,j (ξ)∥2/(2 τ 2)

2πτ 2 .

(A70) The density ratio at observation i is 

qs,i (ξ) = fτn (ucal ,i ; ξ)

fτb (ucal ,i ; ξ) + εcam 

, (A71) where εcam > 0 prevents division by zero. Each Gaussian kernel has unit integral, so the ratio compares concentration at an observation with concentration in its neighborhood. We jointly vary the rotation and camera center to minimize 

Lcam (ξ) = − 1

n

> n

X

> i=1

log  1 − α + αq s,i (ξ), (A72) with fixed α = 0 .1.

Numerical evaluation. We approximate the densities by bilinear rasterization, normalized Gaussian convolution and bilinear evaluation at the observations. The raster stores projected counts without division by their total, and we add 

εcam = 10 −12 to the broad density in raster-count units. At each smoothing stage, the raster covers the extended rect-angle that defines Jcam . We discard poses with fewer than eight projected points contributing to the raster. The coarse stage evaluates a fixed random sample of at most 65,536 model points, and the medium and fine stages evaluate one of at most 262,144. 

Local refinement. At each center, we keep up to eight of the 64 rotations of lowest coarse objective, at pairwise angles above 20 ◦. We refine the kept candidates of a level in order of increasing objective, at most 2,048, 4,096 and 8,192 of them at the three full grids and 2,048 at each further level, in batches of 256. Refinement uses the quasi-Newton method of Broyden, Fletcher, Goldfarb and Shanno (BFGS) [57], jointly updating the rotation and camera center through three smoothing stages. The coarse, medium and fine stages use narrow widths τn = 0 .04 , 0.015 and 0.00375 and broad widths τb = 0 .16 , 0.08 and 0.08 on rasters of 128 × 128 ,

256 × 256 and 1,024 × 1,024 , respectively. They run 15, 20 and 20 iterations, and the fine stage refines the 32 candidates of a batch with the lowest medium objective. These widths are in calibrated image coordinates, while the center grid uses model-coordinate units scaled by the scene extent. 

Three-point correction. For the four candidates of a batch with the lowest fine objective, we pair every observation with its nearest projected model point in the observed image region. We take the 24 closest pairs with distinct model points and solve the three-point problem [39] for every triple of them. We rank the solutions by the medium objective and evaluate the best eight by the fine objective. The best of these replaces the candidate when it lowers the fine objective. 

Refinement by EM. The observed image region is the rect-angle spanned by the 0.5th and 99.5th percentiles of the calibrated observations, enlarged by 5% of each side length on both sides. Each candidate selects the model points at positive depth whose projections lie inside this region. At most 262,144 of these points, drawn at random, form the model for EM and the alignment check, and at least eight are required. EM in its form with outliers then refines the pose twice, from noise levels of 0.03 and then 0.01 in calibrated image coordinates, each time with the CPU refinement of Tab. A3, before the alignment check of App. B.1. 

Acceptance and finer grids. After EM, we accept a pose when it passes the alignment check and at least 

max {8, 0.3 min( n, N )} observations are matched, where 

N counts all model points of the scene. A pose with this many matches is also accepted when the fraction of observa-tions within 10 −3 of a projected model point in the observed image region, in calibrated image coordinates, exceeds by at least 0.2 its largest value at 32 random poses. These random poses have centers drawn uniformly from the enlarged box and rotations drawn from the rotation grid. The search stops at the first accepted pose and otherwise moves to the next candidate. If no pose is accepted after the three full grids, up to three further levels halve the cell size but subdivide only the 512 cells with the best scan scores. If all levels are used, the search returns the pose with the most matched 2D points and reports that it was not accepted. 38 A.12. Coverage of the camera-center grid 

Camera search supplies the initial poses that EM refines (App. A.11). It places candidate camera centers at the centers of a grid of cells in a box around the 3D model. We show how fine the grid must be for some candidate to lie within a given distance of the true camera center, and how many candidates this requires.

## B. Implementation details 

B.1. Search, refinement & acceptance 

Hardware. The implementation requires one Intel Xeon (Cascade Lake) central processing unit (CPU), 128 GB of random-access memory (RAM) and one RTX 4090 graphics processing unit (GPU). This appendix details the four steps of Sec. 4.2 and then the complete search of Algorithm A1, with its settings and complexity. Steps 1 and 2 work in calibrated image co-ordinates, and Steps 3 and 4 measure residuals, distances and noise in pixels. The solver also takes as input whether outliers may be present. This choice selects one of two searches, each organized in stages. In a GPU stage, the GPU generates initial camera poses and fits them by moment matching. It then runs EM on a batch of candidates, and the CPU reruns EM on at most three of them. Without outliers, a CPU search follows the GPU stages if none of their fits is accepted. App. B.2 adapts the steps to edge observations. 

Camera initialization. When the observations cover an unknown part of a scene reconstruction, Step 1 searches over camera centers and orientations for the initial poses. Each candidate pose selects the model points that project into the observed image region, and these points form the model for Steps 3 and 4; further details in App. A.11. 

Monomial features. On outlier-free inputs, the monomial feature map is ϕ4 ◦ h, where h is the fixed image normal-ization of Sec. 4.1. For a point z = ( z1, z 2), the monomial vector lists the fourteen monomials of degrees one to four,

(A74) so its average over a point set contains the fourteen distinct entries of the moments M1 to M4 of Eq. (A1). The averages over the observations and over the projections at a pose ξ are

with density 1/|DF| and model outliers on the bounding box 

B ⊂ R3 of the model points with density 1/ vol( B). The box must have positive volume. We compute the image av-erage μU,0 once by midpoint quadrature on DF. The image of a uniform distribution on B depends on the pose and is generally not uniform, so we recompute μY,0(ξ) at each pose from the projections of a midpoint lattice of B. Lattice points at nonpositive depth also contribute zero. Lemma A6 gives the identity behind Eq. (2). When the inlier obser-vations equal their projections and both point sets contain the same fraction η of uniform outliers, the expected feature averages at the true pose ξ differ by η(μU,0 − μY,0(ξ)) .Noise and unequal outlier fractions add a further residual at the true pose. EM then refines the pose from the individual observations and re-estimates their outlier fraction. 

Camera initialization and moment matching. On the point-set benchmarks, Step 1 places initial cameras at the orientations of a rotation grid, with translations from the cen-troids and spreads as described below, and scores them by 

LF at the lowest cutoff. Whole-scene queries use the camera search of App. A.11. Step 2 proceeds as in Sec. 4.2. Mo-ment matching minimizes LF of Definition 1 with outliers and its special case L4 on complete inputs. To initialize the translation at a grid rotation, we match the projected centroid and root-mean-square radius to those of the observations, in closed form under scaled orthography [27] and then by Gauss–Newton [57] under perspective, keeping every model point in front of the camera. Scaled orthography provides these starting values, and all objectives and refinements use the pinhole projection of Sec. 3. Tab. A3 gives the grid sizes, the numbers of retained poses and their minimum separa-tions. With outliers, we set the outlier fraction at each pose to the least-squares value 

ˆη(ξ) = ⟨d, d0⟩∥d0∥2 ,

d = μU,F − μY,F (ξ),

d0 = μU,0 − μY,0(ξ),

(A75) clipped to [0 , 0.75] . We score each pose by the residual ∥d −

ˆηd0∥2 that remains after subtracting the estimated outlier contribution. Uniform outliers shift the centroids and root-mean-square radii from which we solve for the translation at a grid rotation. Before solving, we therefore subtract the expected contribution of a fraction η0 of uniform outliers on DF from the moments of the observations. We make the same correction to the moments of the model points using the midpoint lattice of B. Uniform outliers fill every cell of a 5×

5 grid on DF, so the emptiest cells contain mainly outliers. We set η0 to 25 /n times the mean count of the four emptiest cells, clipped to [0 , 0.75] . If the fitted fraction at the best pose differs from η0 by more than 0.05 , we repeat the search from that fraction. The fits then proceed through increasing frequency cutoffs KF . If no fit is accepted, the search with outliers repeats this fitting from the further starting fractions of Tab. A3. It finally obtains further camera poses from the rotation grid, optimizes their features and refines them by EM.

From this initial pose, we fit the six pose parameters to L4, with the same normalization h for the observations and the pro-jections. The fit weights the nine real values equally, whereas The-orem A1 measures moment differences in the norm of the full symmetric tensors. For two normalized 2D point sets, the differences of their tensors and the differences ∆ca,b of their complex moments are related by 

∥∆M3∥2 = 14 |∆c3,0|2 + 34 |∆c2,1|2,

∥∆M4∥2 = 18 |∆c4,0|2 + 12 |∆c3,1|2 + 38 (∆ c2,2)2.

The residual for the nine fitted values and the tensor resid-ual therefore vanish at the same poses but weight nonzero differences differently. The residual ˆϵ, the error ϵ and the modulus ΓT of Theorem A1 use the tensor norm. With four model points and positive definite covariances, the normalized model is a regular tetrahedron. Each of its 24 orthogonal symmetries Qs maps it to itself, so the orientations O and OQ s give the same moments and the affine fit cannot distinguish them. For each symmetry, we therefore undo the normalization and fit a rigid pose under the pinhole projection model. The CPU search compares all of these poses within its limits, even if an earlier pose is accepted. For any number of model points, if none of the first affine initial poses is accepted, the CPU search continues on successively finer rotation grids. At each grid rotation, the translation is fitted to the centroid and spread of the observations as in Step 1, which keeps every model point in front of the camera. It then tries the remaining affine initial poses. 

Planar point models. We treat a 3D point set as numeri-cally planar when the smallest eigenvalue of its covariance is at most 10 −8 times the largest. Normalization would then divide the coordinate along the plane normal by a near-zero standard deviation, so the affine fit uses plane coordinates. Let Qp ∈ R3×2 have orthonormal columns along the two leading covariance directions, and let ν be their unit normal. We fit A2 ∈ R2×2 from centered plane coordinates to the calibrated observations. Let κ1 be the largest singular value of A2, and let ap ∈ R2 satisfy apa⊤ 

> p

= κ21I2 − A2A⊤ 

> 2

. We complete A2 to the two matrices A = A2Q⊤ 

> p

± apν⊤. Then 

AA ⊤ = κ21I2, so both completions are scaled-orthographic cameras. They project points that lie exactly in the plane identically, so we compare them by perspective fitting and refinement on the original 3D coordinates. 

EM likelihood. At a pose ξ, let N+(ξ) be the number of model points in front of the camera with finite image projec-tions, ˆs > 0 the current noise standard deviation per image coordinate in pixels, pd the probability that each of these model points is observed, and ncl the expected number of outlier observations. Step 3 models each observation u as an outlier, uniform on a rectangle Dim , with probability η, or as a projection of one of these model points, chosen uniformly, with isotropic Gaussian noise:

A side of zero length is given the length 10 −300 pixels, so the fixed area |Dim | in square pixels is positive. EM evaluates only the projections near each observation. Without outliers, pd = 1 and ncl = 10 −3n stay fixed, and this small expected outlier count keeps a single gross error from dominating the likelihood. EM starts from the noise level of Tab. A3 and anneals it. While ˆsEM < ˆs, each iteration sets ˆs′ = min {ˆsEM , 0.9ˆ s}. At the first iteration with ˆsEM ≥ ˆs, annealing ends, and from then on ˆs′ = ˆ sEM ,bounded on the GPU by the noise ceiling of Tab. A3. When a pose is given without a noise level, such as an initial pose of the CPU search or an alternative of Step 4, EM starts from the root-mean-square distance of the observations to their nearest projections divided by √2, without annealing. EM has converged when annealing has ended, when |ˆs′ − ˆs| ≤ 

10 −4 ˆs, and when no projection coordinate moves by more than 10 −4 ˆs between iterations. 

Valid fits & ranking. Step 4 considers only valid fits. A valid fit has finite parameters and, without outliers, positive depths for all model points. With outliers, every model point with a positive responsibility must stay in front of the camera, and at least three model points must lie in front of the camera with finite image projections. Before CPU refinement, we rank a stage’s valid fits, without outliers first by convergence and then by likelihood. With outliers, we rank them by likelihood alone and, if some of them have 

nm ≥ max {8, 0.3 min( n, N )}, rank only those, because a fit that explains few observations provides little evidence about the pose. If no fit is accepted, the solver returns the valid fit of highest likelihood and reports that it was not accepted. It reports failure when no valid fit remains. 

Association level. For the association level of Definition 2, we use ℓ = min {8, N + − 1} neighbors and measure the distances di,k in pixels. For observation i, the transformed distance ratio is Ei = −ℓ log(1 − d2

> i, 1

/d 2

> i,ℓ +1

). Lemma A7 proves that each Ei has the unit exponential distribution under the Poisson model of Definition 2. Without outliers, the association level is the lower tail 

passoc = Pr 

h

GΓ ≤

> n

X

> i=1

Ei

i

,GΓ ∼ Gamma( n − 3, 1) .

The reduced shape n − 3 accounts for the six fitted pose parameters. With outliers, we count the observations with 

Ei ≤ π/ 16 . When the nearest projection is much closer than the (ℓ + 1) th, the ratio Ei is close to πd 2 

> i, 1

times the density of projections, so the condition places the nearest projection within about a quarter of the average spacing of the projections, the inverse square root of that density. Under the Poisson model, each observation meets it with probability 1 − e−π/ 16 , and the association level is the upper tail passoc = Pr NB ≥ |{ i : Ei ≤ π/ 16 }| ,NB ∼ Binomial( n, 1 − e−π/ 16 ).

These reference distributions treat the Ei as independent. Neighboring observations share projections, and the pose is fitted to the same observations, so neither distribution is exact (App. A.5). We therefore use passoc as a score and compare it with fixed thresholds far in the tail. A pose that EM has not yet refined has no parameters fitted to the distances. The CPU search and the comparison of alternative poses therefore test such a pose by p0assoc , which uses the Gamma shape n without outliers and equals passoc with outliers. 

Step 4: alignment check. The empirical acceptance cri-teria of Algorithm A1 test whether the observations have projections of their own. When the noise is small compared with the spacing of the projections, an inlier observation at the true pose is typically much closer to its generating pro-jection than to any other. The ratio di, 1/d i, 2 of the distances from observation i to its two nearest projections is then typi-cally small, as is the association level. On outlier-free inputs, a further test asks whether the moment residual L4 is no larger than noise of standard deviation ˆs would make it at the true pose. Let zj = h(pj ) be the normalized projec-tions, ∂ϕ4 the 14 × 2 Jacobian of ϕ4, and fK = √K11 K22 

the geometric-mean focal length. With the radius rcal of Sec. 4.1, we approximate the first-order expected value of 

L4 at the true pose by 

V4 = ˆs2

f 2 

> K

r2cal N 2

> N

X

> j=1

∥∂ϕ4(zj )∥2. (A80) 42 When the focal lengths differ, this approximation uses one noise scale in place of the two calibrated-coordinate noise scales. Without outliers, we accept a fit in either of two ways. The first requires convergence, at least 90% as many distinct nearest projections as distinct observation locations, and a median ratio di, 1/d i, 2 over the distinct observation loca-tions of at most 0.25 . Repeated observations at one location share their nearest projection, so both quantities count each location once. With fewer than thirty distinct observation locations, the first way requires all nearest projections to be distinct and replaces the median ratio by its 75th percentile with the same threshold. The second way accepts a pose whose correspondences are not resolved because the noise is comparable to the spacing of the projections, provided the observations lie far closer to the projections than chance would place them. It applies to converged fits with n ≥ 48 .It requires passoc ≤ 10 −14 , relaxed to 10 −12 for n ≥ 200 ,together with L4 ≤ 9V4 unless passoc ≤ 10 −30 .With outliers, we accept a converged fit with nm ≥

max {8, 0.3 min( n, N )} and passoc ≤ 10 −9 if its median ratio di, 1/d i, 2 over the matched observations is at most 0.12 .Matched observations are those to which the correspon-dences of Algorithm A1 assign a model point. For n ≥ 500 ,where dense projections can prevent resolved correspon-dences, an association level of at most 10 −100 allows a me-dian ratio of at most 0.25 . With or without outliers, when the standard deviation of the image noise is given as an optional input, acceptance also requires ˆs to be at most three times that value. 

Step 4: pose ambiguity resolution. Step 4 compares the selected pose, the accepted fit of highest likelihood, with the alternative poses described in Sec. 4.2 and characterized by Lemma A8. We build the alternative poses from selected model points, which are all model points without outliers and, with outliers, those associated with matched observa-tions. Each alternative keeps the centroid of the selected points fixed in camera coordinates. The seven half-turn alternatives of Lemma A8 are the poses RH k for k = 1 , 2, 3 and DRH k for k = 0 , . . . , 3,with Hk taken about the principal axes of the selected points. We form them when at least three points are selected and their principal axes can be computed. Each must have valid projections and p0assoc ≤ 10 −3. Without outliers, we omit alternatives whose rotations differ by less than 3◦ from the accepted pose, fit the others to the image moments, and keep those whose residual is at most the larger of the accepted pose’s residual and 9V4, with V4 evaluated at the alternative. The planar alternative uses the plane through the centroid of the selected points whose normal is a covariance eigen-vector of smallest eigenvalue. It reflects the selected points through this plane, and it reflects the camera coordinates through the plane that contains the centroid and is parallel to the image plane. The two reflections compose to the ro-tation DRH k, with Hk the half-turn about that eigenvector, so this alternative starts from the half-turn alternative that Lemma A8(c) identifies for planar models. It differs from that alternative in its refinement. For a planar model, the accepted pose and its alternative give nearly the same image with the same correspondences, so we first fit this alternative under perspective projection to the correspondences of the accepted pose. We then refine it by EM only if its projec-tions are valid, its rotation differs by more than 3◦ from the accepted pose and p0assoc ≤ 10 −3.After EM, we discard alternatives of either kind whose rotation differs by less than 3◦ from any accepted pose. A remaining alternative replaces the selected pose only if it satisfies the acceptance criteria and has higher likelihood. The selected pose then gives the correspondences as in Al-gorithm A1. 

Correspondences. At the returned pose, each matched ob-servation corresponds to its nearest projection, and every other observation is an outlier. When several observations share a nearest projection, only the closest keeps it. With outliers, an accepted pose with at least six matches is refit-ted to these correspondences by least squares, and the refit replaces it if the matched points stay in front of the camera. 

Search stages. Algorithm A1 expands Algorithm 1 with the search stages of the point-set benchmarks, the division of work between the GPU and the CPU, the early exits and the acceptance criteria, and Tab. A3 gives the settings of each stage. Inputs must have finite coordinates and meet the requirements of Sec. 3 and the point-count limits in the table. The table states noise levels in units of ρrms , the root-mean-square distance of the observations from their centroid, in pixels. The search stops after the first stage that yields an accepted fit. A fit that is not accepted but has an association level at most 10 −100 ends the GPU stages early, because its pose already places the observations far closer to the projections than chance would. Without outliers, the CPU search then follows. It defers initial poses with 

p0assoc > 10 −3 and, if none of its other initial poses reaches EM, refines up to three deferred poses from a larger noise level. 

Complexity. For fixed numbers of features, candidates and iterations, computing the features costs O(n + N ). A GPU EM iteration then costs O(n + N + Npair ), where Npair 

counts the observation–projection pairs that the GPU exam-ines in a grid of square image cells and can reach O(nN ).Sorting the observations into these cells adds O(n log n),and GPU storage is O(n + N ) for fixed batch size and grid limits. CPU refinement takes O(N log N + nN ) time per iteration and can store O(nN ) neighbor pairs. With fixed search and iteration limits, the solver therefore has worst-case time O(nN + ( n + N ) log( n + N )) and space O(nN ).43 Algorithm A1: MomentPnP, expanding Algorithm 1; GPU lines handle all poses at once, CPU lines one at a time

Moment matching and EM use every input point. Scoring the rotation grid uses at most 4,096 model points, and cross-correlation at most 1,024. The CPU search has no time limit. 

Stage Settings Inputs Outlier-free: n = N ≥ 4. With outliers: min( n, N ) ≥ 6, and EM needs at least three model points in front of the camera with finite image projections. Outlier-free search We use 800 × 48 rotations, refined to 3,200 × 96 in the second stage, and keep at most 1,024 or 2,048 initial poses separated by 6◦. Moment matching uses 30 iterations, keeping at most 96 or 192 fits separated by 1◦.Fourier moment matching 200 × 24 rotations scored with KF = 4 and ζF = 0 .15 , keeping 48 initial poses separated by 10 ◦. Five fitting stages use (KF , ζ F ) = (4 , 0.15) , (8 , 0.08) , (12 , 0.05) , (16 , 0.04) and (20 , 0.03) , and fit the best 48, 20, 20, 8 and 8 poses for 20, 12, 12, 10 and 10 iterations. Padding δF = 0 .02 and taper width τF = 0 .06 . The image average μU,0 uses 

48 × 48 midpoints of DF, and μY,0 uses an 8 × 8 × 8 midpoint lattice of B. The second stage starts from the outlier fractions 0, 0.15, 0.30, 0.45 and 0.60. Other poses with outliers 800 × 48 rotations. Translations come from centroids and spreads corrected for observed outlier fractions 0, 0.05, 0.1, 0.15, 0.2, 0.3, 0.4 and 0.5 and model outlier fractions 0, 0.2 and 0.4, keeping the best score per rotation, or from cross-correlation at seven scales from 0.5 to 2 times the ratio of the spreads. Each translation estimate keeps 2,048 initial poses separated by 6◦, and after 16 GPU EM iterations the 256 of highest likelihood continue. GPU EM At most 120 iterations. Without outliers, initial noise 0.02 ρrms and ceiling 0.05 ρrms . With outliers, initial noise and ceiling 0.1ρrms . The batch stops when its fit of highest likelihood has converged, counting with outliers only fits with 

nm ≥ max {8, 0.3 min( n, N )}, and the other fits stop early. Fits whose estimated noise reaches 0.999 times the ceiling have not found the observations and do not go to CPU refinement. CPU refinement We refine at most three candidates per stage, selected by the ranking rules of Step 3. Without outliers, fits stopped early receive at most 60 CPU EM iterations, and converged fits or fits at the iteration limit go to the acceptance criteria without further EM. With outliers, we first run 30 CPU EM iterations. If the fit has not converged and its association level is at most 10 −3, we run at most 60 further iterations. CPU affine search Planar point sets (covariance eigenvalue ratio at most 10 −8) use both completions of each distinct planar affine fit from the closed-form initialization and 720 initial rolls. Other point sets use 3,000 viewing directions and 72 rolls, and the nine real values of the complex moments. We initially try six fitted orientations and increase this to at most 48 if no estimate satisfies the acceptance criteria. CPU rigid search 200 × 24 , 800 × 48 and 3,200 × 96 rotations, keeping 24, 64 and 128 initial poses separated by 10 ◦. A finer level requires its rotation count times N ≤ 4 × 10 7. Other initial poses on the CPU use at most 600 EM iterations, and the final attempts initialized with a larger ˆs use 150 with initial noise 0.05 ρrms .1k 16k 256k

Figure A1. Measured time and memory on rendered ETH3D scans. (a) Time per frame. Light markers are single views and lines are medians. The triangle marks the Courtyard view that returned no pose within its 1,800-second limit. (b) GPU allocator memory, which is equal across the views of each size, and the peak CPU memory of the four one-million-point runs that recorded it (gray). The horizontal line is the memory of the specified GPU. 

Measured time and memory. The complexity bounds above are worst cases. In Fig. A1, we measure time and memory on the rendered ETH3D scans of App. C.5, from 1,000 to one million model points, with every model point or a quarter of them observed. GPU memory grows linearly with the number of model points. From 64,000 points on, the GPU allocator memory grows by about 12.4 KiB per added point when every model point is observed and reaches 11.87 GiB at one million points. With a quarter observed, it holds about 115 KiB per model point, or 7.0 GiB at 64,000 points. CPU memory stays small, and the peak resident memory of the four one-million-point runs that recorded it is 0.81 to 1.11 GiB. Time grows faster than memory. Up to 16,000 points, the median time per frame grows about linearly with the number of points. From 64,000 to 256,000 points, it grows about as the square of that number. This growth is consistent with the term Npair , because denser projections in a fixed image place more of them in each grid cell around an observation. At one million points, the seven completed views take 689 to 8,735 s, with a median of 1,244 s. 

Where the solver stops. No run of MomentPnP exhausted memory. Up to one million points, the only failure is one Courtyard view that returned no pose within its 1,800-second limit (App. C.5). The input size is therefore limited by time. Linear extrapolation of the measured memory places the limit of a 24 GiB GPU near two million points with every model point observed and near 200,000 model points with a 45 quarter observed. The learned baselines, by contrast, run out of memory from 16,000 or 64,000 points (Tab. A17). 

B.2. Edge observations 

In the study of App. C.8, we reduce a red–green–blue (RGB) image and a computer-aided design (CAD) mesh to straight edges. The image edges are line segments found by a de-tector. In the mesh, an edge is a crease when the normals of its two adjacent faces differ by at least 30 ◦ or when it has a single adjacent face. An edge is a contour at a candidate pose when one adjacent face is turned toward the camera and the other away from it. At a candidate pose, the model edges are the visible creases and contours. We extract the image edges and the model edges independently. The inputs thus contain no pairing between image and model edges, and the two edge sets may sample the same visible boundary at different densities. The method uses these edges in two forms. For moment matching, it averages the features over image arclength, the length along the edges in the image, rather than over points. Perspective projection shortens the parts of a 3D edge that are farther from the camera, so a uniform average along the 3D edge would not match an average along the detected image edge. Lemma A19 shows that the two image-arclength feature averages agree at the true pose when the detected edges coincide with the visible projected edges. For Step 3, EM needs point observations. We place the 2D observations at equal image-arclength spacing along the detected image edges. At each initial pose, we place points at equal image-arclength spacing along the visible projected model edges and map each one back to its 3D point on the edge by inverting the change of parameter in the proof of Lemma A19. These 3D points form the model points and stay fixed during refinement. Because the two sets of samples are placed independently, an observation and a model point need not correspond to the same physical point, and EM treats them as two unordered point sets.

These are displacement by the detector, edges present in only one of the two sets, and quadrature error. The lemma then turns the resulting feature discrepancy into a bound on pose error near the true pose. A coupling makes the comparison precise without pairing edges. It is a joint distribution of a point on the true projected edges and a point on the observed im-age edges, each distributed uniformly with respect to image arclength. 

Lemma A20 (Edge mismatch & local pose stability) . We couple the arclength distributions on the true projected edges and the observed image edges, using a joint distribution with these two marginals. We assume that the coupling places at least a fraction 0 ≤ γE ≤ 1 of its mass on pairs at image distance at most δE ≥ 0. We let the fixed measurable feature map have norm bound B ≥ 0 and Lipschitz constant 

Lip( ϕ) ≥ 0 on a bounded image region containing the observed edges and the projected edges at all poses in a fixed neighborhood of ξ⋆. We let ˆμE and ˆμC be quadrature 

46 approximations of μE and μC , and we let ϵq bound their combined image and model quadrature error throughout that neighborhood. The empirical feature discrepancy at the true pose then satisfies 

∥ ˆμE (ξ⋆) − ˆμC ∥ ≤ ϵE = γE Lip( ϕ)δE + 2 B(1 − γE ) + ϵq .

(A82) 

For a pose chart ξ(δ) in fixed six-dimensional local co-ordinates, we define gE (δ) = μE (ξ(δ)) . We assume that gE is continuously differentiable and that its Jaco-bian J⋆ ∈ Rdϕ×6 at the true pose coordinates δ⋆ has full column rank, with least singular value κJ > 0. An esti-mate ˆδ sufficiently close to the true pose, with fitted residual 

ˆϵE = ∥ ˆμE (ξ(ˆδ)) − ˆμC ∥, satisfies 

∥ˆδ − δ⋆∥ ≤ 2(ˆ ϵE + ϵE )

. (A83) 

Proof. We first bound the difference of the exact averages through the coupling. On at least a fraction γE of the cou-pled mass, the two points lie within δE of each other, so their features differ by at most min(Lip( ϕ)δE , 2B). On the remaining mass, the features differ by at most 2B.Since min(Lip( ϕ)δE , 2B) ≤ 2B, a larger γE can only lower this bound. Integration over the coupling therefore bounds the difference of the exact averages by ϵlaw =

γE Lip( ϕ)δE + 2 B(1 − γE ). Adding the quadrature errors by the triangle inequality gives Eq. (A82). At the estimate, the triangle inequality through ˆμE (ξ(ˆδ)) , then ˆμC and then 

μC gives ∥gE (ˆδ) − gE (δ⋆)∥ ≤ ˆϵE + ϵq + ϵlaw = ˆ ϵE + ϵE .We next show that gE separates poses near δ⋆. We write

γE of arclength that the coupling pairs within δE contributes at most γE Lip( ϕ)δE . The remaining fraction 1 − γE , which includes arclength present in only one of the two edge sets, contributes up to 2B(1 − γE ). Since ϵE ≥ 2B(1 − γE ),the bound is small only when nearly all arclength is shared. Eq. (A83) converts the feature discrepancy into a pose er-ror, and the factor 2/κ J is large when the features respond weakly to some pose change. The bound is local and applies only to an estimate that the search has already brought near the true pose. ## C. Experiments 

This appendix follows the order of Sec. 5. App. C.1 gives the benchmark inputs and the learned baselines, and each later subsection gives the protocol and full results of one study in the main experiments section. 

C.1. Datasets & baselines 

This subsection describes the inputs of the three benchmarks in Tab. 1 and how we run the learned baselines on them. 

Benchmark inputs. For MegaDepth [46], ModelNet40 [79] and Data61 [56], we read the released per-frame 2D and 3D coordinates of the blind PnP benchmarks [6] and keep their original point selection. MegaDepth pairs 2D points with model points that COLMAP reconstructs by structure from motion (SfM). Its test frames contain 10 to 12,125 points. ModelNet40 samples 1,000 points uniformly from each CAD model, projects them with a virtual pinhole cam-era and perturbs the projections with Gaussian image noise. Data61 pairs panoramas with light detection and ranging (LiDAR) point sets, and we use its released frames of 1,000 points. We remove the repeated padding entries indicated by the released counts, leave all other coordinates unchanged and shuffle both point sets. Each test set supplies 1,000 ran-dom outlier-free frames and 300 frames with at most 2,000 inliers. To the latter, we add outliers drawn uniformly in the bounding box of each point set. We call these frames the comparison frames. The outlier ratio is the number of outliers added to each point set divided by its inlier count. 47 Learned baselines. We run Deep Blind PnP [48], BP-nPNet [6] and GoMatch [82] with their released code and weights. BPnPNet returns three poses, from RANSAC on its predicted correspondences, from its declarative layer and af-ter Levenberg–Marquardt (LM) refinement. On each dataset, we run BPnPNet checkpoints trained with the correspon-dence loss alone ( Lc) and with the pose loss. The Deep Blind PnP release passes the 2,000 most probable matches to the P3P-RANSAC of OpenCV. We also evaluate posest, the pose-estimation library recommended in that release, which reproduces its reported MegaDepth median rotation error. The released variant with a fixed match count fails on frames with fewer than about 45 points, because these frames have fewer than 2,000 candidate pairs. Our top-n variants there-fore pass the 1.5 min( n, N ) most probable matches, up to 2,000. The variants marked +LM refine the RANSAC pose by LM on its inliers. For GoMatch, we apply its coordinate-only MegaDepth checkpoint to the input point sets without retrieved reference images. Tab. A6 lists every pose output that we evaluate, and Tab. 1 reports the most accurate one. 

C.2. Benchmarks 

This subsection completes the benchmark comparison of Tab. 1. It reports error quartiles and distributions, accuracy at every outlier ratio and for every pose output, runtimes, results on held-out frames, and examples. 

Errors without outliers. Tab. A7 gives the quartiles of rota-tion error and of relative translation error on the outlier-free test sets, together with the percentage of frames within both 

1◦ and 1%. Fig. A2 plots the full rotation-error distribution on each test set, and Fig. A3(c) the distribution of relative translation error. A symmetric object can have a large ro-tation error at a correct alignment, so on ModelNet40 we also measure ADD-S [30]. It averages the distance from each model point under the estimated pose to the nearest model point under the reference pose. ADD-S is below 10% of the model diameter on 99.8% of all 2,466 outlier-free ModelNet40 test frames. 

Outliers. Tab. A6 gives the accuracy of every evaluated pose output at each outlier ratio, at thresholds of 1◦ and 

5◦. Fig. A3(a) plots accuracy against the outlier ratio, and Fig. A3(b) the rotation-error distribution with as many out-liers as inliers. 

Runtime. Tab. A4 gives the median time per frame at each outlier ratio. Without outliers, GoMatch alone is faster than MomentPnP. With outliers, MomentPnP is the slowest of the four methods, at 0.2 to 7.1 s per frame. Fig. A4 plots accu-racy against time, comparing methods that return a single pose with searches that improve their best pose until the time limit. Tab. A5 groups the outlier-free MegaDepth test frames by their number of points. In every group, 99.8 to 100% of frames are within 1◦. C.3. Role of moment matching 

This subsection gives the protocols and full results of the three comparisons in Sec. 5 that test what moment matching contributes to MomentPnP. They match moments of dif-ferent orders before the same EM refinement, restart EM from random poses, and search globally over poses. The subsection ends with point sets smaller than those covered by Theorem 1.

put, uses the same search effort and fits translations from the centroid and spread of the 2D points. We use 100 outlier-free test frames from each benchmark and a separate sample of thirty independent synthetic models of 64 points. Their pro-jections receive Gaussian image noise of standard deviation 

0.000625 per calibrated coordinate. The moment variants match the averages of all five, nine or fourteen nonconstant monomials through degree two, three or four. All orders share one image normalization. Each moment variant ranks and refines poses by its own moment residual. Direct EM takes the initial poses in a fixed random order. All variants retain four poses with the same minimum angular separa-tion, refine them with the same EM settings and select the final pose by likelihood. For the counts before EM, we es-timate the noise and mixture parameters and select a pose by likelihood without updating it. Before EM, the third-and fourth-order variants bring 53 to 78 of the 100 poses on each benchmark within 1◦ rotation error and 1% relative translation error, and the second-order variant at most two (Tab. A9). After EM, both still recover more poses than direct EM and the second-order variant on every dataset. Which of the two is more accurate varies across datasets. 

EM from random poses. EM-ICP in Tab. 1 restarts the EM refinement of MomentPnP from random poses until the 30-second limit and keeps the most likely pose. It restarts a median of 210 times per MegaDepth frame, and its 30 seconds per frame are over five hundred times the median 50 MomentPnP Deep Blind PnP BPnPNet CFPE (reproduced) GoMatch EM-ICP 0 1412 1

time of MomentPnP without outliers (Tab. A4). Fig. A4 shows how its accuracy grows with time. 

Global search. GOPAC [7] and GOSMA [5] search over poses by branch and bound within a given domain of camera centers. We run them and MomentPnP on thirty complete, outlier-free pairs of point sets, ten fixed frames from each of the MegaDepth, Data61 and ModelNet40 test sets, without the released time limits. We remove the external clock and the internal duration limits of the released programs, 6,000 s for GOPAC and 600 s for GOSMA, and otherwise keep their code and settings. A search then ends on its own only when GOPAC proves its optimum or GOSMA reaches its released queue-size limit. within the earlier limits, the modified and released programs return the same poses. We stopped the GOPAC searches still running after 5.2 to 6.0 hours and scored their best pose so far. In a first attempt, four GOPAC searches exceeded a per-process memory cap of 3 GB, and we restarted them without the cap. Both global methods receive unit bearings and the same normalized model points. We center each model at its 3D centroid ¯y and scale it to a fixed root-mean-square distance from that centroid. For GOPAC, we use the released angular tolerance of 0.017 radians ( 0.974 ◦) and compare two initial lower bounds on the number of inliers, zero and an annealed bound that starts from one fewer than the number of 2D points. GOSMA minimizes a normalized squared L2

distance between densities, and we keep its released stopping tolerance of 0.1 on the gap between the best objective value and the global lower bound. 

Search domain. Both global methods need a bounded do-main of camera centers that contains the true center, which we compute from the inputs. Let amax be the largest distance from a model point to the centroid ¯y, let c be the camera center, and let β > 0 be the angle between two observed unit bearings. The bound below holds for every such pair, and we take a widely separated pair because a larger angle tightens it. For noiseless complete observations, the camera distance dc = ∥c − ¯y∥ satisfies 

dc ≤ amax 

 1 + csc( β/ 2) .

The bound is immediate when dc ≤ amax . When dc >amax , the two model points observed along these bearings are at least dc − amax > 0 from the camera, so by the cosine rule they are at least 2( dc − amax ) sin( β/ 2) apart. They are also at most 2amax apart, which gives the bound. Both global methods search the same cuboid centered at ¯y, with half-width twice the right-hand side of the bound to allow for measurement noise. This cuboid contains all thirty reference camera centers. 

Global search results. We evaluate the final pose of each finished search and the best pose so far of each stopped one, in the original coordinates. Timings include program startup, input processing and model preparation. Tab. A10 reports every outcome. MomentPnP recovers all thirty frames within 

1◦, and its largest errors are 0.753 ◦ in rotation and 0.303% 

in relative translation. Neither global method brings a frame within 1◦. GOSMA ends every search at its queue-size limit, after a median of 69 s. In each setting, GOPAC finishes twelve of the thirty searches, on ten ModelNet40 and two MegaDepth frames, and none of the Data61 searches. In the two settings, the finished searches take a median of 43 and 50 minutes. Every finished GOPAC search certifies a pose at which every observation is an angular inlier, with rotation errors from 5.4◦ to 179 ◦. The number of angular inliers that GOPAC maximizes therefore does not identify the pose. On a 113-point MegaDepth frame, for example, both settings certify 113 angular inliers at rotation errors of 

19 .689 ◦ and 8.552 ◦. For these poses, all 113 model points lie in front of the camera, and the maximum nearest-neighbor angular errors are 0.812 ◦ and 0.882 ◦, below the tolerance of 

0.974 ◦. An inaccurate pose can therefore make every point an angular inlier. Since every finished search is at least 5.4◦

off, the one annealed GOPAC pose within 5◦ comes from a stopped search. 52 Table A10. Global search on complete inputs without the released time limits, all outcomes. Small point sets. Theorem 1 assumes at least twenty points, so we also evaluate MomentPnP on smaller com-plete inputs. For each of N = 8 and N = 16 , we draw one model whose 3D coordinates are independent and Gaussian with mean zero and standard deviation 0.2. We then apply a fixed pose, project with identity intrinsics and independently shuffle the exact projections. The global methods keep the parameters and normalization above and search all rotations, with the camera center in the fixed box [−5, 5] 3 in model units. Here, unlike above, GOSMA stops at a 30-second search limit. On both models, the unchanged solver recov-ers the generating pose within 1◦ on every run, with lower median rotation error and runtime than either global method (Tab. A11). Repeated GOPAC runs can return different poses with the same maximal number of angular inliers. GOSMA reaches its search-time limit on both models. 

C.4. Further datasets 

Tab. 2 tests whether MomentPnP, with its benchmark set-tings, recovers poses from structure-from-motion inputs of four further datasets. This subsection describes the recon-structions, image selection and preprocessing for these in-puts. It then gives the errors and runtimes per dataset, the comparison with outliers on all images, and examples. 

Reconstructions. For Cambridge Landmarks [40], we use the VisualSFM reconstructions [78] of King’s College, Old Hospital, Shop Facade and St. Mary’s Church. For ETH3D [65], we use the released reconstructions of Court-yard, Delivery Area, Electro, Facade, Office and Terrace. For Phototourism, we use the COLMAP reconstructions [64] of the Image Matching Challenge [37] for Trevi Fountain, Brandenburg Gate and Sacr´ e-Cœur. For 7-Scenes [68], we use the depth-assisted SfM models of Sarlin et al . [62] for Heads, Fire and Chess. 

Queries. Each query contains the tracked 2D points of one image and the model points observed in that image. We undistort the 2D points, keep the tracks with positive depth and reprojection error below three pixels, and shuffle both point sets. On Cambridge Landmarks, we take the four test images with the most points in each scene and eleven further test images spread over the rest. On the other datasets, we take up to twelve landscape images per scene with 100 to 3,000 points, or up to 4,000 points on 7-Scenes and 6,000 on ETH3D. With outliers, we keep the images with at most 2,000 inliers and add half as many outliers as inliers to each point set, as in the benchmark protocol. The learned methods keep their MegaDepth weights, and we report the released output with the most poses within 1◦.

Results. Tab. A12 extends Tab. 2 with the median rotation error and runtime of each method on each dataset. Tab. A13 removes the limit of 2,000 inliers and evaluates all 169 im-ages with outliers. On these inputs, MomentPnP returns 167 poses, 147 of them within 1◦. GoMatch runs out of memory on nine images, and the other baselines return a pose for every image. 

Examples. Fig. A7 shows the first selected outlier-free im-age of each dataset for every method. Fig. A8 adds images from Cambridge Landmarks, Phototourism and Data61, and Fig. A9 from 7-Scenes and ETH3D. MomentPnP is within 

1◦ on every image and has the lowest rotation error on all but one. On the Chess image of Fig. A7, CFPE and EM-ICP are 0.01 ◦ lower. In each figure, exactly two images have a pose within 1◦ from at least one learned method. 53 0.04 ◦

C.5. Scaling to large point sets 

This subsection gives the protocol behind Figs. 7 and 8 and the results per scan and per view up to one million points. It also reports the percentage of correct labels, the runtimes and the results of the learned baselines. 

Rendered 2D points. The experiment varies the number of points, and the fraction of model points that generate 2D points, on real scene geometry. We use the ETH3D laser scans [65] of Facade, Courtyard and Office, which hold 45.0, 37.8 and 48.6 million scan points. For each scan, three registered cameras give three reference poses, and 2.6 to 10.6 million scan points fall within the view frustum of a reference pose. Within this frustum, we sample nested subsets of the scan points with positive depth as model points and project them to form the 2D points, ignoring occlusion. We add independent isotropic Gaussian noise with standard deviation s = 1 pixel per coordinate in a 6,200 × 4,100 

image and shuffle both point sets. In a second setting, with partial detection, a uniformly sampled quarter of the model points generates 2D points. Every method receives the same point sets, and the learned baselines use their MegaDepth weights. Times cover complete inference runs and exclude input and model loading. MomentPnP runs a small GPU warm-up before timing. The times of the learned methods include compilation on first use where it is required. The Courtyard and Office runs have a limit of 1,800 seconds per view and point count. 

Pose accuracy. Tab. A14 gives the median rotation error, percentage of correct labels and time over the three reference poses of each scan. Fig. A10(b) plots the median rotation error of MomentPnP on each scan, together with the learned baselines over all nine views. At one million points, all three Facade runs return poses, and one of them takes 8,735 57 0.02 0.1 0.5 2050 100

Correspondences. Each 2D point is labeled with its nearest projected model point. When the noise is small compared with the spacing between nearby projections, nearly every label is correct. This spacing shrinks as the point sets grow, so more 2D points lie nearer to another projection than to their own. Fig. A11 plots the median percentage of 2D points whose nearest projected model point under the re-covered pose did not generate them. The Labels columns of Tabs. A14 to A16 give the percentage that did. The percent-age of correct labels falls from 100% at 1,000 points to 75 to 79% at one million. Over the same range, the rotation error decreases up to 256,000 points and stays near that level at one million (Tab. A14). App. A.9 relates these percentages to the probability that every label is correct. 

Runtime. Fig. A12 gives the median time per frame of MomentPnP on each scan and compares it with the learned baselines. Across the scans, the median time of MomentPnP is 0.09 to 0.14 s at 1,000 points and 84 to 355 s at 256,000 points (Tab. A14).

C.6. Complete scene reconstructions 

We evaluate MomentPnP on observations in one image and the complete 3D reconstruction. Neither the visible model points nor a pose prior is given. App. A.11 specifies the camera search and the refinement by EM. Here we give the query protocol, the results of Tab. A18 and the search cost. 

Queries. We use the thirteen ETH3D high-resolution train-ing scenes of Tab. A18. Each query contains the tracked 2D points of one image and every finite model point of the released sparse reconstruction [65], which starts from COLMAP [64], with both point sets shuffled. We exclude image detections without a triangulated track. In each scene, the tuning queries are the first three images in file-name order with at least 200 tracked 2D points, and we fix the settings on them. We test the settings on the test queries, three further images spaced evenly over the remaining eligible images. A query is recovered when the returned pose has rotation error below 1◦. App. A.11 specifies the camera search and its settings. 

Results. Tab. A18 gives the recovery in each scene for the tuning and test queries, and for the learned baselines on the tuning queries. MomentPnP recovers all 39 tuning queries within 1◦, with rotation errors of at most 0.015 ◦, and 35 of the 39 test queries. Fig. A13 plots the number of tuning queries recovered by each search time. The search recovers 19 of the 39 within 43 s and the last after 3,531 s. 59 Table A18. Recovery from full scene reconstructions on ETH3D. 

Each query contains the tracked 2D points of one image and every finite SfM model point of the scene, without the visible subset or a pose. Points is the number of model points, and Ratio is the scene extent divided by the viewing distance. Recovery columns count queries with rotation error below 1◦. The learned baselines and Tuning use the three queries on which we chose the settings, and Test uses three held-out queries with these settings fixed. DBP, BPnP and GoM denote Deep Blind PnP, BPnPNet and GoMatch, and OOM means out of memory. Figure A13. Search time on complete scene reconstructions. 

Number of the 39 tuning queries of Tab. A18 whose search has returned a pose within 1◦ by a given search time. 

Cost of the search. The number of grid centers that the search must visit sets its cost. Let dv > 0 be the viewing distance, the depth of the observed part of the scene. Sup-pose that the true camera center lies in the enlarged box of App. A.11 and that, for some fraction rc > 0, refinement succeeds from any grid center within rcdv of it. Suppose also that the rotation grid covers the basin of convergence. By Lemma A18, cells of side ∆c = 2 rcdv/√3 place some grid center within rcdv of the true camera center, and the grid has at most Q

> k

(√3Lk/(2 rcdv) + 1) centers, where Lk

are the side lengths of the enlarged box. The bound grows roughly as the cube of the ratio of scene extent to viewing distance. Recovery also requires that the search keep this grid center after ranking. At each center, scoring the 11,520 rotations takes work linear in the number of model points in the coarse sample, which is at most 65,536. Beyond the three full grids, the search subdivides only the best cells. For the 70 complete grids used by the tuning queries in Tab. A18, the cell containing the reference camera center ranks in the top quarter in 80% of grids. Four queries, on Electro and Playground, continue to selective subdivision. For evalua-tion, the viewing distance is the median depth of the model points observed in the query image, and the Ratio column of Tab. A18 divides the scene extent by it. 

C.7. Ablation study 

This subsection gives the protocols and results of the controls in the ablation study of Sec. 5, which vary the depths of the model points, the image noise and the kind of outliers. It then reports three further controls, on the search implementation and the pose ambiguity resolution, on the selection of visible points and on the acceptance test on held-out frames. 

Depth variation. The affine initial poses of Step 1 assume an affine camera (Theorem A1), and a perspective camera departs further from an affine camera as the depths of the model points spread. In each paired trial of Tab. A19, we vary the depths about a reference depth zref = 4 .5 while keeping the noise-free image bearings and the noise real-ization fixed. In the first set of conditions, the depths are 

zref (1 + fsp qsp ,j ) for fixed qsp ,j ∈ [−1, 1] and a spread fac-tor fsp . In the second, 13 of the 128 points move closer to the camera and the other depths stay at zref . We draw calibrated image coordinates independently from scaled and shifted Beta distributions. The first set draws qsp ,j uniformly from 

[−1, 1] and uses fsp ∈ { 0.02 , 0.1, 0.3, 0.6, 0.9}. The second places the same 13 points at 0.8, 0.5, 0.2 and 0.1 times zref 

in separate trials. The affine initialization in this study tests 3,000 viewing directions with 72 rolls each, keeps the affine map with the lowest residual and converts it to an initial pose under scaled orthography, without perspective fitting or EM. The affine initial pose has rotation error above 1◦ in every trial. After perspective fitting and EM, the full solver is within 1◦ in 88 of the 90 trials. At the largest spread, with a median depth ratio of 18, the affine initial pose has a median error of 161 ◦, and the full solver is within 1◦ in all ten. As the spread shrinks, the median error of the full solver rises from 0.05 ◦ to 0.37 ◦. Its two inaccurate poses, at 1.0◦

and 1.3◦, are accepted and occur at the smallest spread. 

Image noise. These synthetic controls measure how image noise affects the pose and the labels. We draw N model points independently and uniformly from [−0.1, 0.1] 3. The rotation axis is uniform on the unit sphere, and the rotation angle is uniform in [0 , π ]. The translation is (tx, t y , 1) ⊤

We add in-dependent isotropic Gaussian noise with standard devia-tion s ∈ { 0.02 , 0.25 , 0.5, 1, 2} pixels per coordinate. Each noise level uses 200 trials for N ∈ { 16 , 32 , 64 }, 100 for 

N ∈ { 128 , 256 } and 40 for N = 1024 . In Theorem A4, ex-act matching depends on the noise through N s relative to the spread of the points. Let ˆs be the noise standard deviation fit-ted by MomentPnP, and let ρrms = ( n−1 P

> i

∥ui − ¯u∥2)1/2

be the root-mean-square radius of the 2D points about their mean ¯u, both in pixels. To compare point counts on one scale, we plot results against N ˆs/ρ rms . Over this noise range, the rotation error of MomentPnP follows that of the pose fitted to the true correspondences. Its percentage of correct labels follows that of minimum-cost bijective match-ing at the true pose (Fig. A14(a)). The second agreement is consistent with Lemma A9, which bounds the labeling error of nearest-projection matching at an accurate pose. It is also consistent with Theorem A7, under which, for large N , even an estimator that knows the camera errs with probability bounded away from zero once N s is at least a fixed positive constant. 

Exact label recovery. A stricter measure is the percentage of frames in which every 2D point is labeled with its gener-ating model point. Fig. A15 plots it for MomentPnP, CFPE and EM-ICP against N ˆs/ρ rms , and Fig. A10(a) against the noise at N = 1024 . EM-ICP restarts on the CPU for up to 30 s. At N = 1024 and s = 0 .02 pixels, MomentPnP recovers every label in all 40 frames, compared with 90% of the frames for CFPE and 30% for EM-ICP. At N = 1024 

and s = 0 .5 pixels, none of these methods recovers every label in any frame.

Structured outliers. Real outliers often lie on scene struc-ture, which the uniform outlier model of Definition 1 does not describe. To test such outliers, we take Data61 frames with N model points and replace a fraction of the 2D points of each frame with as many other 2D points on the same scene structures. MomentPnP is within 1◦ on every frame with up to 30% replaced and on 79% to 99% of the frames at 50% (Fig. A14(b)). At every fraction, BPnPNet is within 

1◦ on fewer frames.

The rows on pose ambiguity resolution count the frames in which an alternative pose of Step 4 is examined and the frames in which it replaces the selected pose (Lemma A8). These replacements occur mainly in the CPU search, on flat MegaDepth scenes and ModelNet40 objects. Both replace-ments in the GPU search and 61 of the 62 in the CPU search are within 1◦. Alternatives within 3◦ of an accepted pose are discarded, so each of these replacements corrects a pose at least 2◦ from the true one. Model symmetry has a related effect. Under exact symmetry, different poses give identi-cal projections, and near-symmetry can make the moment residual less informative about orientation error (Apps. A.4 and A.7). On ModelNet40, 27 of the 31 frames with rotation error of at least 5◦ meet the ADD-S criterion of 10% of the model diameter (App. C.2), as do 11 of the 13 accepted with such errors. The last two rows count the errors of the accep-tance test in both directions. The test accepts 13 ModelNet40 poses with rotation error of at least 5◦ and none on the other test sets. It declines 7 MegaDepth and 60 ModelNet40 poses within 1◦.

Selection of visible points. Moment matching needs both point sets to sample the same structure, so recovery can de-pend on which model points are visible as well as on how many. We compare two selections in paired trials on thirty independent 3D models of 96 model points (Tab. A21). Each pair uses the same 3D point set, intrinsics, pose, measure-ment noise and unmatched 2D points. The noise is Gaussian with standard deviation 0.001 per calibrated coordinate. In both selections, 24 model points generate 2D points. Ran-dom selection chooses them uniformly at random, and di-rectional deletion chooses them from one end of the model along a random image direction. Uniform unmatched 2D points then bring the 2D point set to n points. With n ≤ 48 ,random selection gives 26 to 29 of the 30 poses within 1◦
