Acceptance on held-out frames. Fig. A14(c) splits the 1,000 held-out frames of each dataset and outlier ratio by acceptance and by rotation error below 1◦. In every setting, at most 8 frames yield an accepted pose with rotation error of at least 1◦.

C.8. Independently selected inputs 

In every study above, each inlier 2D point comes from a model point, through a track, a released pair or rendering. In the studies of this subsection, the 2D points and the model points are selected independently, so no 2D–3D correspon-dences are given. The studies pair scale-invariant feature transform (SIFT) detections with a 3D model built from other photographs, image edges with a CAD model, and detected motion-capture markers or infrared light-emitting diodes (LEDs) with their 3D positions. The SIFT study uses the search over camera centers and rotations of App. C.6. The study with CAD models uses the edge features of App. B.2. The marker studies apply MomentPnP to the two point sets. Tab. A22 summarizes recovery in these studies, with a SIFT example in Fig. 9(a) and a CAD example in Fig. A16. 

SIFT detections & reconstructed maps. We use the ETH3D scenes Office, Courtyard and Facade. In each scene, the first photograph is a tuning query, and two photographs evenly spaced through the scene’s list of photographs are test queries. We compute new SIFT detections [49] and 63 triangulate the model points [64] from source photographs that exclude all nine queries, so no query photograph takes part in feature extraction, matching or triangulation. The Of-fice, Courtyard and Facade maps contain 3,272, 42,714 and 96,909 model points from 23, 35 and 73 source photographs. The search uses the grids of App. C.6, with three subdivision levels. 

Acceptance. We tune the acceptance thresholds for these inputs on the three tuning queries and fix them before eval-uating the six test queries. A pose is accepted only if at least 30% of the points in the smaller point set are matched. This applies both to the expected number nm of 2D points matched under EM (App. B) and to the final correspon-dences. We require the median, over the matched 2D points, of the ratio of the distances to the nearest and second-nearest projections to be at most 0.12. For queries with at least 500 detections and an association level of at most 10 −100 , we relax this limit to 0.50. The benchmark acceptance test with outliers permits 0.25 in this case (App. B.1). We run the search on each test query with a time limit of 3,600 sec-onds, checked between completed operations. At the limit, it returns its best valid pose. A query is recovered when its rotation error is below 1◦ and its camera-center error below 1% of the scene extent. 

SIFT results. Tab. A23 gives every returned pose. Two of the six test queries are recovered. The accepted Facade 26 pose of Fig. 9(a) gives 5,666 distinct pairs, all in front of the camera. Their median and root-mean-square reprojection distances are 0.763 and 1.168 pixels. The accurate Office 18 pose is returned at the time limit without being accepted. 

Why such inputs are harder. Fig. 9(b,c) shows two mea-surements that explain this difficulty. First, the two point sets can sample different structure. Of the 12,069 SIFT points detected in a Meadow image, 255 belong to reconstruction tracks, and many of the other detections lie on vegetation and sky that the reconstruction does not contain (Fig. 9(b)). Second, a dense 3D model places projections near most 2D points at more than one pose. In a Facade image, 2.6 mil-lion laser-scan points fall within the view. At the reference pose, 80.6% of the 9,754 2D points lie within 3.4 pixels of a projected scan point. At a pose rotated by 2◦, 76.8% still do (Fig. 9(c)). Distances to the nearest projections therefore barely distinguish the two poses. 

Image edges & CAD models. We fix all filter, search and refinement settings on four tuning views of T-LESS [32] object 25. Before evaluating any pose error, we select views 72 and 360 of objects 1, 5, 9, 13, 17, 21, 26 and 29, and views 72, 216, 360 and 504 of object 25. The twenty test views thus contain sixteen views of new objects and four held-out views of the tuning object. Each run receives one RGB image, the intrinsics and a CAD mesh in its own coordinate frame, and no reference pose, visibility, model symmetries or edge identities. 

Edge features & search. We use eight-pixel segments from the line segment detector (LSD) [25] in the image, and creases and candidate contours (App. B.2) on the CAD model. We normalize image coordinates by the arclength-weighted centroid and root-mean-square radius of the de-tected image edges. We integrate Fourier features analyti-cally along each projected segment using sinc functions. We score an initial grid of poses with these features and keep the best poses for EM. Scoring on this grid ignores occlusion. For the retained poses, exact visibility then clips occluded edges, and all CAD vertices must lie in front of the camera and project inside the image. For point-based EM, we sam-ple each projected edge once by image arclength and keep these samples during refinement. For the final ranking, we recompute visibility and sampling and score each pose by an edge-curve likelihood. An improvement of the point-based EM objective thus need not improve this likelihood. 

Edge results. Tab. A24 gives the rotation and camera-center errors of every test view, for the returned pose and after applying the official model symmetry. A view is recovered when its rotation error is below 5◦ and its camera-center error below 50 mm. To find whether a failure comes from the search or from the objective, we score the reference and estimated poses with the same final likelihood, using a Gaussian standard deviation of two pixels and a uniform outlier fraction of 0.05. Every reference pose satisfies the constraints of the search, with every CAD vertex in front of the camera and projecting inside the image. At these poses, we sample the detected image edges and the visible projected CAD edges by image arclength, at most two pixels apart. A median of 72.9% of the image-edge samples and 83.1% of the CAD-edge samples lie within three pixels of a sample of the other set. The detected and projected edges thus overlap only in part. Of the twelve views that fail under model symmetry, four return poses with lower final likelihood than the reference pose, so the search misses a more likely pose. In the other eight, the estimated pose has higher likelihood than the reference, so the objective itself favors these inaccurate estimates. 

Motion-capture markers. We use the static trial of a public running motion-capture dataset. 4 For the query camera, we keep all unlabeled marker centroids, corrected for radial dis-tortion. We tune on one camera at the first frame and evaluate all eight other cameras at frame 501, using MomentPnP with one-pixel noise and a 30-second time limit. Six of the eight returned poses are within 5◦ and 5 cm, and none is within 

1◦ and 1 cm. One of the five accepted poses fails the 5◦ and 5 cm thresholds. As a control, efficient perspective-n-point

(EPnP) [44] with the reference assignments is within 5◦ and 5 cm on all eight cameras and within 1◦ and 1 cm on one. 

Infrared LEDs. We use the raw infrared images and the surveyed coordinates of four noncoplanar LEDs from a pub-lic demonstration [17]. A fixed filter on intensity, area and contour shape extracts the LED centroids, and the point solver runs in its outlier-free setting with one-pixel noise and a ten-second time limit. The transform between the LED model and the attached AprilTag [59] is unknown, so we compare motions relative to frame 61 with indepen-dent tag estimates. With Ξtag

Table A25. Motion-capture markers and infrared LEDs (ours). 

A comparison is accurate when its rotation error is below 5◦ and its position error below 5 cm. For motion capture, these are the errors of the camera pose. For the LEDs, they are the errors of the motion of each of the 31 other selected frames relative to frame 61. Accepted outputs and extraction failures count frames, and inaccurate accepted comparisons count motions. Error medians use all eight motion-capture poses and the 24 LED motions for which both poses are available.
